\log w_{t}^{(i)}\leftarrow\ell(\mathbf{y}_{t}|\mathbf{y}_{1:t-1},{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}},\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}_{1:t})+\log f(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}_{t}|\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}_{1:t-1},\boldsymbol{\varphi})-\log\pi(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}_{t}|\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}_{1:t-1},\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t-1}^{(i)},\mathbf{y}_{1:t})

8:

end for

9:

\widehat{\ell}(\mathbf{y}_{t}|\mathbf{y}_{1:t-1},\boldsymbol{\vartheta},\boldsymbol{\varphi})\leftarrow\log\left(\frac{1}{n_{part}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{part}}w_{t}^{(i)}\right)

10:

\bar{w}_{t}^{(i)}\leftarrow w_{t}^{(i)}/\sum_{j=1}^{n_{part}}w_{t}^{(j)}

11:

resample
\left\{\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}_{1:t},\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t}^{(i)}\right\}_{i=1}^{n_{part}}
according to the weights
\left\{\bar{w}_{t}^{(i)}\right\}_{i=1}^{n_{part}}

12:

end for

In section
7
we explore ways in which the large population theory from section
5
is relevant to the construction and behaviour of PALSMC algorithms for over-dispersed models:

•

It is well known that the efficiency of sequential Monte Carlo methods can be highly sensitive to the choice of the proposal distribution,
\pi
in algorithm
5
. If we could choose
\pi(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}|\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}_{1:t-1},\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t-1}^{(i)}\mathbf{y}_{1:t})
to be proportional (as a function of
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}
) to:

\exp\left[\ell(\mathbf{y}_{t}|\mathbf{y}_{1:t-1},{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}},\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}_{1:t-1},\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t})\right]f(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}|\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}_{1:t-1},\boldsymbol{\varphi}),

(6.9)

then the weight
w_{t}^{(i)}
would have no dependence on
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}^{(i)}
. Consequently the variability of the weight would be reduced and the overall efficiency of the PALSMC algorithm likely improved. This “optimal” choice of
\pi
is often not analytically tractable, but inspired by our consistency theory we suggest Laplace approximation to it. We demonstrate such proposals in simulation-based and real data examples in sections
7.2
–
7.4
and find them to be very efficient in practice.

•

Through a simulation example in section
7.2
, we illustrate that even for our over-dispersed models, where one might expect estimation consistency to be ruled out, increasing population size can in fact increase the accuracy of point estimates of
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}
obtained from the r.h.s. of (
6.8
). The explanation for this is that whilst the model may be over-dispersed once
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1:t}
are integrated out, it is equi-dispersed
conditional
on
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1:t}
.

In the examples in section
7
we also expand on algorithm
5
to include sophisticated resampling schemes and block particle filtering techniques
[
56
]
.

7
Discussion and examples

Code for all examples is available at:
https://github.com/LorenzoRimella/PAL
.

7.1
Inference using automatic differentiation and HMC for an age-structured model of ’flu

In this example, we demonstrate PALs for an age-structured model of a 1957 outbreak of influenza in Wales. Computation is performed using the probabilistic programming language Stan
[
14
]
, taking advantage of automatic differentiation to implement Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC). This example also highlights how the general Latent Compartmental Model can accommodate discrete or discretisable covariates associated with subpopulations: in this case the covariates are indicators of the age-group which individuals belong to and this is reflected in the compartment structure of the model.

Data and Model

The data consist of 19 weeks of incidence data in the form of GP symptom reports for a town with population size
8000
across
4
age groups:
00-04
,
05-14
,
15-44
, and
45+
. The data were analysed by
[
67
]
and are available via the Github page associated with
[
3
]
. For each age group
k=1,\dots,4
,

\displaystyle S_{k,t+1}

\displaystyle=S_{k,t}-B_{k,t},\qquad

\displaystyle E_{k,t+1}=E_{k,t}+B_{k,t}-C_{k,t},

\displaystyle I_{k,t+1}

\displaystyle=I_{k,t}+C_{k,t}-D_{k,t},\qquad

\displaystyle R_{k,t+1}=R_{k,t}-D_{k,t},

with conditionally independent increments:
B_{k,t}\sim{\text{Bin}(S_{k,t},1-e^{-h\bar{\beta}_{k,t}})}
,
C_{k,t}\sim{\text{Bin}(E_{k,t},1-e^{-h\rho})}
,
D_{k,t}\sim\text{Bin}(I_{k,t},1-e^{-h\gamma}),
where

\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\bar{\beta}_{1,t}\\
\vdots\\
\bar{\beta}_{4,t}\\
\end{array}\right]=\underbrace{\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}\beta_{11}&\dots&\beta_{14}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\beta_{14}&\dots&\beta_{44}\\
\end{array}\right]}_{=:\mathbf{B}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}I_{1,t}\\
\vdots\\
I_{4,t}\\
\end{array}\right]\frac{1}{n}.

(7.1)

\mathbf{B}
is a symmetric matrix with element
\beta_{ij}
representing the rate at which two individuals, one from the susceptible compartment of the
i
th age group and the other from the infective compartment of the
j
th age group come into effective contact.

The mean time spent in the exposed compartment
1/\rho
and the mean recovery time
1/\gamma
are taken to be independent of age group and set to be
1.5
days, following
[
3
]
. We assume that the model evolves daily with
h=1/7
and that observations consist of cumulative weekly transitions from the
E
to
I
compartments for each age group, that is we have observation times at times
\tau_{r}=7r
for
r=1,\dots,R
corresponding to the end of each week. In the setting of case (II) we denote observations
\bar{\mathbf{Y}}_{k,r}=\sum_{s=\tau_{r-1}+1}^{\tau_{r}}{\mathbf{Y}}_{k,t}
where each element of each
{\mathbf{Y}}_{k,t}
is equal to zero except the
(2,3)
th element corresponding to transitions from compartment
E
to
I
which, conditional on
C_{k,t}
, is distributed
{Y}^{(2,3)}_{k,t}\sim\text{Bin}(C_{k,t},Q_{k,t}^{(2,3)}),
where
\mathbf{Q}_{k,t}\in[0,1]^{4\times 4}
has elements equal to zero except for the
(2,3)
th entry which is equal to an age group dependant under reporting parameter
q_{k}\in(0,1)
which is to be estimated. We give details of how this model is written as an instance of the Latent Compartmental Model and the algorithm used to calculate the PAL in the supplementary material.

Hamiltonian Monte Carlo with automatic differentiation in Stan

We now consider MCMC sampling to approximate the posterior
p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\bar{\mathbf{Y}}_{1:R})
.
The probabilistic programming language Stan
[
14
]
provides a framework for implementing HMC – a type of MCMC algorithm which uses auxiliary “momentum” variables to help explore the posterior – in which the user only needs to specify priors and provide a function which evaluates the likelihood for the model. Stan uses Automatic Differentiation (AD) to compute gradients and update the auxiliary HMC variables without the need for user input. Since the PAL consists of recursive compositions of elementary linear algebra operations, it is a natural candidate for AD.

Results

We implemented a Stan program incorporating the PAL, details of which are given in section
C.4
of the supplementary material. We stress that here we do not correct for the fact that the PAL is only an approximation to the true likelihood, so Stan is targeting an approximation to the true intractable posterior, although in a separate example in the supplementary material we explore corrections using Delayed-Acceptance MCMC methods.

The parameters to be estimated are
\boldsymbol{\theta}=[\beta_{11}\;\cdots\;\beta_{44}\;q_{1}\;\cdots\;q_{4}]^{\top}
, the initial state for each age group is assumed known as
\mathbf{x}_{1,0}=[948\;0\;1\;0]^{\top}
,
\mathbf{x}_{2,0}=[1689\;0\;1\;0]^{\top}
,
\mathbf{x}_{3,0}=[3466\;0\;1\;0]^{\top}
,
\mathbf{x}_{4,0}=[1894\;0\;1\;0]^{\top}
. We used vague gamma priors
\beta_{ij}\sim\text{Gamma}(5,1)
for
{i,j=1,\dots,4}
and a vague truncated normal prior
q_{k}\sim\mathcal{N}(0.5,0.5)_{\geq 0,\leq 1}
for
k=1,\dots,4
. The HMC sampler was run to produce a chain of length
5\times 10^{5}
iterations, a burn-inperiod of size
10^{5}
was discarded and the remaining was thinned to produce a sample of
2.5\times 10^{4}
. We report approximate posterior distributions and trace plots in section
C.4
of the supplementary material, these show no signs of unsatisfactory mixing. Figure
2
reports the posterior predictive distributions and credible intervals, we see good coverage of observed data.

We repeated the analysis using an ODE version of the same age-structured SEIR model, from
[
3
]
, with a Poisson reporting model: we use as emission distribution a Poisson distribution with rate given by the ODE solution scaled by an under-reporting parameter. This was implemented in the Stan framework using the code available in
[
3
]
, we again sampled a chain of length
5\times 10^{5}
iterations, discarded a burn-inperiod of size
10^{5}
, and thinned the remaining to produce a sample of
2.5\times 10^{4}
. To calculate the reproduction number
R_{0}
for stratified models such as this, one must calculate the so called
next generation matrix

[
66
]
which has elements given by
\frac{n_{i}\beta_{ij}}{n\gamma}
where
n_{i}
is the population size of the
i
th age group.
R_{0}
is then given by the largest modulus of the eigenvalues of the next generation matrix
[
19
]
. Using this definition, we can produce approximate posterior distributions of
R_{0}
using each of the PAL and ODE procedures, which we report in figure
1
. The approximate posteriors concentrate around
1.42
using the PAL and
1.82
using the ODE model. This disparity in estimates can be related to the features of the posterior predictive distributions reported in figures
2
and
3
: the distribution of trajectories in figure
3
appears to ‘overshoot’ the data in comparison to those in figure
2
, reflecting the higher force of infection implied by the ODE procedure in contrast to the PAL procedure. These posterior predictive plots also exhibit the inherent inflexibility of the ODE model: since the latent process is deterministic, random variations in the data away from the ODE trajectory must be explained as observation error. As is apparent in the
45+
age group, this rigidity in modelling results in overconfidence and a poor fit compared to that of the stochastic model combined with the PAL procedure.

Figure 1:
Age-structured ’flu example. Approximate posterior distributions for
R_{0}
under the PAL and ODE procedures.

Figure 2:
Age-structured ’flu example. Posterior predictive distributions obtained from inference under the stochastic model using the PAL within Stan.

Figure 3:
Age-structured ’flu example. Posterior predictive distributions obtained from inference under the ODE model using Stan.

7.2
Pedagogical over-dispersed SEIR example

To demonstrate inference for an over-dispersed model using PALSMC we consider a simple SEIR model for which the latent population
\mathbf{x}_{t}\equiv[S_{t}\;E_{t}\;I_{t}\;R_{t}]^{\top}
evolves according to transition matrix (
3.1
), with immigration and emigration parameters,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{t}
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}
, combined with the observation model
y_{t}\sim\text{Binom}(I_{t},q_{t})
. We assume
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{t}
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}
are known. We can cast this model in the form discussed in section
6
by identifying
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}=[\beta\;\rho\;\gamma]
,
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1:T}=q_{1:T}
, and choosing
f(\cdot|\boldsymbol{\varphi})
to make
q_{1:T}
i.i.d. according to a truncated normal distribution
q_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{q},\sigma^{2}_{q})_{\geq 0,\leq 1}
, with
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=[\mu_{q}\;\sigma_{q}^{2}]
,
\mu_{q}\in[0,1]
and
\sigma^{2}_{q}>0
. We give the details of a PALSMC scheme for this model in section
C.1
of the supplementary material, including the design efficient, data-informed proposals by Laplace approximation to (
6.9
), inspired by the theory from section
5
.

Figure 4:
Pedagogical over-dispersed SEIR example. Top two rows: filtering distribution approximations and ESS obtained from PALSMC with
n_{part}=10^{4}
particles and increasing model population size
n
. Bottom row: maximum PALSMC estimation of hyper-parameters
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=[\mu_{q}\;\sigma_{q}^{2}]
over increasing time horizons. Each boxplot summarises
100
hyper-parameter estimates.

Filtering and parameter estimation simulation study

To assess the ability of the PALSMC scheme to recover ground truth quantities, we simulated data from the model with
[\beta\;\rho\;\gamma\;\mu_{q}\;\sigma_{q}^{2}]=[0.8\;0.1\;0.2\;0.5\;0.1]
,
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{0}=[0.99\;0\;0.01\;0]^{\top}
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{t}=0.05\boldsymbol{\pi}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}=[0.95\;0.95\;0.95\;0.95]^{\top}
. The first two rows of figure
4
explore the performance of PALSMC with increasing population size
n
and using the data-generating values of
[\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\;\boldsymbol{\varphi}]
. This collection of plots was created by first sampling a single draw of latent variables
q_{1:100}\sim f(\cdot|\boldsymbol{\varphi})
, then for each value of
n=10^{3},10^{4},10^{5}
, generating data
\mathbf{y}_{1:100}
from the model conditional on
q_{1:100}
, and running the PALSMC algorithm. We see that the effective sample size (ESS) is high across all values of population size
n
, indicating a good approximation to the r.h.s. of (
6.6
); this reflects the careful choice of proposal distribution. As in (
6.8
), for each
t\geq 1
, the PALSMC algorithm yields a Monte Carlo approximation
p(q_{t}|y_{1:t})\approx\sum_{i=1}^{n_{part}}\bar{w}_{t}^{(i)}\delta_{q_{t}^{(i)}}
. The first row of plots in figure
4
demonstrates that these PALSMC filtering approximations concentrate on the true
q_{1:t}
as the population size
n
grows. This is in keeping with the theory of section
5
, which tells us that
\text{argmax}_{q_{1:t},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\ell(y_{1:t}|q_{1:t},\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
converges to the data generating
[q_{1:t}\;\boldsymbol{\vartheta}]
in the large population limit
n\rightarrow\infty
.

We also explored the ability of the procedure to recover the data generating hyperparameters
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=[\mu_{q}\;\sigma_{q}^{2}]
; in the bottom plots of figure
4
. Here each boxplot summarises
100
estimates, each estimate was obtained as follows: (1) simulate
q_{1:450}\sim f(\cdot|\boldsymbol{\varphi})
and data
\mathbf{y}_{1:450}
from the model with population size
n=10^{4}
, (2) construct a 2-dimensional grid of candidate values for estimation of
[\mu_{q}\;\sigma_{q}^{2}]
, (3) run PALSMC with input
\mathbf{y}_{1:450}
for each grid point, with
n_{part}=10^{4}
particles and
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
set to the DGP, (4) at time-steps
t=50,100,150,...
report as an estimate of
[\mu_{q}\;\sigma_{q}^{2}]
the value on the grid for which the largest value of
\widehat{\ell}(y_{1:t}|\mu_{q},\sigma_{q}^{2},\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
was obtained across the PALSMC runs. We see from these boxplots that, for increasing time horizon
T
, the maximum PALSMC estimators obtained across
100
simulations converge towards the data generating
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
with little bias.

Overall, these simulation results illustrate that, even in an over-dispersed setting, a large population can be useful in estimating
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1:t}
, whilst a large time horizon can be useful in recovering hyperparameters
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
.

7.3
Comparison of over-dispersion mechanisms in a model of rotavirus

In this section we explore a model selection task in which an equi-dispersed model is nested within a larger class of models including over-dispersion, using the approach of section
6
. The rotavirus data and model we consider are inspired by
[
63
]
, who assessed the fit of a family of continuous time, stochastic models with varying degrees of over-dispersion using the Akaike Information Criterion (AIC).

Models

The data considered consist of weekly incidence counts of rotavirus infections in Germany for
3
age groups over the
8
year period
2001
-
2008
. We consider a discrete-time version of the model of
[
63
]
which compartmentalises a population of
n=82,372,825
into an age stratified SIR model
\{S_{k,t},I_{k,t},R_{k,t}\}_{k=1}^{3}
comprising
3
age groups:
0-4
,
5-59
, and
60-99
. Given the number of susceptibles in age group
k
at time
t
after immigration, which we denote
\bar{S}_{k,t}
, and the number of infected individuals in each age group
\boldsymbol{I}_{t}=[I_{t,1}\;I_{t,2}\;I_{t,3}]^{\top}
, for
t\geq 1
the number of new infected individuals in each age group
k=1,2,3
at time step
t
is conditionally distributed:

B_{k,t}\sim\text{Binom}\left(\bar{S}_{k,t},1-\exp\left\{-\frac{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}^{\top}\boldsymbol{I}_{t}}{n}\chi_{t}\right\}\right),

(7.2)

where
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}={[\beta_{k}\;\beta_{k}\;\beta_{k}]^{\top}}
with
\beta_{k}>0
denotes the force of infection experienced by age group
k
, and
\chi_{t}={\left(1+\rho\cos(2\pi t/w+\phi)\right)}
denotes a deterministic seasonality component with amplitude
\rho\in[0,1]
, phase
\phi\in[0,2\pi]
, and period length
w>0
, which we set to correspond to
1
year. Other details of the latent compartmental model are given in section
C.5
of the supplementary materials. We assume an aggregated transmission model, with weekly observations coming at times
\tau_{r}=4r
for
r=1,\dots,R
. For each age group observations are conditionally distributed
Y_{k,r}\sim\text{Binom}\left(\sum_{t=\tau_{r-1}}^{\tau_{r}}B_{k,t},q_{k,r}\right)
.

We consider three variants of this model:

EqEq:

a fully equi-dispersed model, in which
q_{k,r}=\mu_{q}\in[0,1]
, and
\mu_{q}
is assumed known as in
[
63
]
;

EqOv:

an equi-dispersed latent compartmental model and an over-dispersed observation model, the same as EqEq except that
q_{k,r}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{iid}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{q},\sigma^{2}_{q})_{\geq 0,\leq 1}
where
\sigma^{2}_{q}>0
is to be estimated;

OvOv:

over-dispersion in both the latent and observation models, the same as EqOv except that we augment
\chi_{t}
in equation (
7.2
) to
\chi_{t}\xi_{r}
where for
r\geq 1
,
\xi_{r}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d}}}{{\sim}}\text{Gamma}(\sigma_{\xi},\sigma_{\xi})
are multiplicative disturbances with mean
1
and
\sigma_{\xi}>0
is to be estimated.

Inference

The parameters we estimate in each instance of the model are given by: EqEq:
\boldsymbol{\theta}=[\beta_{1}\;\beta_{2}\;\beta_{3}\;\phi\;\rho]
; EqOv:
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}=[\beta_{1}\;\beta_{2}\;\beta_{3}\;\phi\;\rho]
with
\{\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{r}\}_{r\geq 1}=\{\mathbf{q}_{r}\}_{r\geq 1}
and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\sigma_{q}
; OvOv:
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}=[\beta_{1}\;\beta_{2}\;\beta_{3}\;\phi\;\rho]
with
\{\bar{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}_{r}\}_{r\geq 1}=\{[\mathbf{q}_{r}\;\xi_{r}]\}_{r\geq 1}
and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=[\sigma_{q}\;\sigma_{\xi}]
. The PALSMC algorithm for this model is given section
C.5
in the supplementary material. For parameter estimation the approximate likelihoods of each of the models EqEq, EqOv, OvOv, obtained from PALSMC were maximised using a finite-difference coordinate ascent algorithm; we ran the optimisation
100
times, initialised randomly over a range of feasible values. Plots evidencing convergence are in section
C.5
of the supplementary materials. The algorithm was implemented using R and Rcpp on a node of the University of Bristol’s BluePebble cluster, although we did exploit parallelization.

We note that a PAL, e.g. the exponential of the r.h.s. of (
4.8
), is a valid likelihood function associated with a product of vector-Poisson distributions whose intensity parameters are defined through the corresponding filtering algorithm, e.g. algorithm
1
. Similarly the output from PALSMC, e.g. (
6.8
) from algorithm
5
, is a Monte Carlo approximation to a valid likelihood for a mixture of products of vector Poisson distributions. This validity justifies the use of AIC for model comparison but with the log-PAL, or the log-output from PALSMC, substituted in place of the usual log-likelihood.

As a benchmark comparison, we fitted an ARMA(2,0,1) model to the log-transformed data, which gives an AIC of 23043 (details are given in the supplementary materials section
C.5
). Table
1
gives the AIC values for each of our models, along with the best AIC value reported by
[
63
]
, which was for a model with over-dispersion in the transition model in the form of multiplicative gamma distributed noise, and over-dispersion in the observation model through negative-binomial reporting. This model of
[
63
]
is therefore qualitatively most similar to our model OvOv. The average computation times were calculated over
100
runs of the coordinate ascent procedure. We find that, whilst we can fit EqEq with high computational efficiency, our two over-dispersed models achieve a substantially better AIC score, indicating a much better fit with increasing over-dispersion. Both EqOv and OvOv outperform
[
63
]
AIC and computation time, although of course the latter is implementation-dependent. Figure
5
demonstrates the increase in goodness of fit that an over-dispersed model provides for the rotavirus data, we see that prediction intervals for OvOv drastically outperform those for EqEq in terms of coverage.

The estimated values of
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
we find for all three models EqEq, EqOv and OvOv (table
2
) are quite similar to those reported by
[
63
]
, but we find a slightly lower value of
\beta_{3}
. For EqOv and OvOv we find a similar seasonal amplitude
\rho
but slightly larger phase
\phi
than
[
63
]
. The seasonal
R_{0}
ranges for each model are: EqEq
(0.98,1.027)
,EqOv
(0.83,1.22)
, and OvOv
(0.82,1.14)
compared to
(0.855,1.152)
obtained by
[
63
]
. The better fit of EqOv and OvOv compared to
[
63
]
may thus be attributed to some combination of quite subtle differences in estimates of parameters related to disease transmission, together with the difference between the negative binomial observation model in
[
63
]
and the way EqOv and OvOv treat the
q_{k,r}
as latent variables.

0-4
corresponding to
1000
realisations of OvOv (top panel) and EqEq (bottom panel), using maximum PALSMC parameter estimates.

7.4
Evaluating the role of unit-specific parameters in a meta-population model of measles

In this section we illustrate how the PAL framework can be used to calibrate a more complex, larger-scale model, and compare the fit of sub-models with different levels of unit-specific parameters.

Model

We consider a discrete time version of a measles model originally presented by
[
71
]
, subsequently extended into a spatio-temporal framework by
[
29
]
and recently explored by
[
55
]
using guided intermediate resampling filter (GIRF) techniques.

The model describes the evolution of recurrent pre-vaccination measles epidemics in
J=40
cities across the UK over the
15
year period
1950-1965
. The model has susceptible
(S)
, exposed
(E)
, infective
(I)
, and removed
(R)
compartments for each of the
J=40
cities. For each city
k=1,\dots,J
the initial state of the epidemic is given by
[S_{k,0}\;E_{k,0}\;I_{k,0}\;R_{k,0}]^{\top}\sim\text{Mult}(n_{k,0},\boldsymbol{\pi}_{k,0})
, where the probability vector
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{k,0}
is a possibly city -specific initial distribution parameter, and
n_{k,t}
for
t\geq 0
denotes time varying population size. For each city
k=1,\dots,J
the population evolves twice per week with the following dynamic:

\displaystyle S_{k,t+1}

\displaystyle=S_{k,t}-B_{k,t}-F^{(S)}_{k,t}+A_{k,t},\qquad

\displaystyle E_{k,t+1}=E_{k,t}+B_{k,t}-C_{k,t}-F^{(E)}_{k,t},

\displaystyle I_{k,t+1}

\displaystyle=I_{k,t}+C_{k,t}-D_{k,t}-F^{(I)}_{k,t},\qquad

\displaystyle R_{k,t+1}=R_{k,t}+D_{k,t}-F^{(R)}_{k,t},

where
F^{(\cdot)}_{t,k}
and
A_{k,t}
model emigration (deaths) and immigration (births), respectively; and
C_{k,t}
and
D_{k,t}
are binomially distributed (details in the supplementary material). The term
B_{k,t}
represents the number of new infections in the
k
th city and is distributed

B_{k,t}\sim\text{Bin}\left(S_{k,t}-F^{(S)}_{k,t},1-e^{-hb_{k,r}}\right),

where:

b_{k,r}={\beta_{k,r}\xi_{k,r}\cdot\left[\left(\frac{I_{k,\tau_{r}}}{n_{k,\tau_{r}}}\right)+\sum_{l\neq k}\frac{v_{k,l}}{n_{k,\tau_{r}}}\left\{\left(\frac{I_{l,\tau_{r}}}{n_{l,\tau_{r}}}\right)-\left(\frac{I_{k,\tau_{r}}}{n_{k,\tau_{r}}}\right)\right\}\right]},

(7.3)

for
r\geq 1
,
t=\tau_{r},\dots,\tau_{r+1}-1
. Here
\beta_{k,r}
denotes a possibly city-specific seasonal transmission coefficient and
\xi_{k,r}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{iid}}}{{\sim}}\text{Gamma}(\sigma_{\xi},\sigma_{\xi})
, for
\sigma_{\xi}>0
, is mean-
1
multiplicative noise which achieves over-dispersion in the marginal distribution of
B_{k,t}
.

The summation term in (
7.3
) encodes the intercity interaction under a ‘gravity model’ – see
[
65
]
for background on these kind of models in epidemiology. The strength of the interaction
v_{k,l}
is computed as:

v_{k,l}=g\frac{\bar{s}}{\bar{n}}\frac{n_{k,0}n_{l,0}}{s_{k,l}},

where
g
is called the ‘gravitational’ constant parameter,
\bar{n}
is the average of the initial populations,
\bar{s}
is the average inter-city distance and
s_{k,l}
denotes the distance between cities
k
and
l
. The interpretation of the gravity model is thus that the strength of the interaction between two cities is directly proportional to their populations and inversely proportional to their distance.

The observations are aggregated incidence data in the form of cumulative fortnightly transitions from infective to recovered for each of the
40
cities, at times
\tau_{r}=4r
for
r=1,\dots,R
. Our observation model, which allows for over-dispersion, is described in section
C.6
of the supplementary material, along with the distributions of
C_{k,t}
,
D_{k,t}
,
F^{(\cdot)}_{k,t}
, and
A_{k,t}
, and an explanation of how we write the model as an instance of the Latent Compartmental model with
h=3.5
days, corresponding to bi-weekly transitions.

We consider three variants of this model all with over-dispersion in both the dynamics and observation mechanisms, but with increasing levels of city-specific parameters:

A:

the initial distribution vectors
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{k,0}
and force of infection parameters
\beta_{k,r}
are shared across cities, i.e. constant in
k
;

B:

\boldsymbol{\pi}_{k,0}
is city-specific and
\beta_{k,r}
is shared across cities;

C:

\boldsymbol{\pi}_{k,0}
and
\beta_{k,r}
are city-specific.

Here we are inspired by an investigation conducted by
[
32
]
, where sub-models with increasing numbers of city-specific parameters were fitted to a dataset on a smaller spatial scale, comprising
20
cities compared to the
40
we consider.
[
32
]
suggested that approximation techniques may be needed to analyse larger data sets, our application of the PAL framework is a step in that direction. However we note that the
20
-city dataset analysed by
[
32
]
is not a subset of the
40
-city dataset we consider here, so direct comparisons of model fit may not be made. Never-the-less we shall compare our results to those obtained by
[
55
]
for a model in which parameters are shared across cities, fitted to the same
40
-city dataset we consider.

Inference

In section
C.6
of the supplementary material we give the details of a PALSMC algorithm in which the PAL is embedded within a block particle filter
[
56
,
32
]
, to numerically approximate the log-likelihood. We used data-informed proposals and lookahead resampling to improve efficiency. For each of the models A,B,C, the approximate log-likelihood obtained from this PALSMC algorithm with
5000
particles was maximized with respect to the model parameters through Sequential Least Squares Programming. The procedures were implemented using Python and TensorFlow on a 32gb Tesla V100 GPU available on the HEC (High-End Computing) facility from Lancaster University.

Table
4
details PALSMC approximate log-likelihood and AIC values for each of the models A,B,C, along with an approximate log-likelihood reported by
[
55
]
for comparison. The GIRF used by
[
55
]
consists of a simulator for a continuous in time latent process combined with a particle filter which uses guide functions for intermediate propagation and resampling, parameters of the model are estimated via an iterated filtering scheme. Together with Monte Carlo adjusted profile methodology
[
31
]
they are able to generate profile likelihood estimates for confidence interval estimation. Frequentist uncertainty interval calculation is out of the scope of the current work and would require results on the asymptotic distribution of the maximum PAL estimator, see section
7.5
for a discussion.

Our model A is similar to that of
[
55
]
in the sense that both these models have parameters shared across cities, but we find model A performs better in terms of log-likelihood and AIC. As we move to from model A to models B and C, by making more parameters city-specific, we see an improvement in log-likelihood and AIC. We also note that the computation time for fitting model A is orders of magnitude smaller than that of
[
55
]
. The computation time is of course implementation-dependent, but we note that we have not devised a bespoke optimization algorithm to maximize the PALSMC approximation, but rather applied a standard ‘black-box’ optimizer. As prompted by an anonymous reviewer, we fitted an ARMA(2,0,1) model to the log-transformed data for a benchmark comparison; this gave a log-likelihood of -69168 (details are given in the supplementary materials section
C.6
).

Estimates of the city-specific parameters
\beta_{k,r}
in model C can be used to estimate city-specific
R_{0}
values, calculated as in
[
32
]
. We find that across the
40
cities these estimated
R_{0}
values lie in the range
5.63-16.65
. The fitted mean latent and infective periods for model
C
were
8.49
and
9.53
respectively; these values are in line with previous inferences on the behaviour of measles epidemics
[
28
]
,
[
18
]
. Full details of our numerical results are in the supplementary materials section
C.6
.

runs of PALSMC with

particles. ‘No. parameters’ is the number of parameters estimated by maximising the log-likelihood for each model.

.

Figure
6
shows projected case numbers for the
4
fortnights following the end of the data record, obtained using model C with parameters fixed to the estimated values, full details are in the supplementary materials section
C.6
. We see a general increase in forecast uncertainty as the time horizon increases, this reflecting the over-dispersed nature of model C. We also see that the forecasts generally exhibit higher certainty for cities with a larger population, as might be expected if a larger sub-population size allows latent variables and parameters which are specific to that sub-population to be estimated more accurately.

Figure 6:
Measles example. Projected case numbers for the
4
fortnights (ordered top-left, top-right, bottom-left, bottom-right) following the end of the data record. For each town/city, the diameter of the outer-most concentric ring represents log-population size. The shade of the outer concentric ring corresponds to the lower
5\%
quantile of the simulated case numbers, the shade of the middle concentric ring to the mean, and the inner concentric ring to the upper
95\%
quantile.

7.5
Opportunities for further research

In the examples from section
7.3
and
7.4
we have not devised special optimisation techniques to estimate parameters, but rather just plugged PALSMC likelihood function evaluations into ‘black-box’ optimisers. There may be opportunities here to even further increase computational efficiency, for example by embedding PALSMC within an iterated filtering scheme
[
30
]
.

Recently
[
34
]
devised sophisticated SMC algorithms to fit agent-based models in which individuals in the population each carry covariates influencing, for example, the probabilities that they come into contact, and hence the probabilities of disease spreading from one individual to the next. When these covariates are discrete and take only finitely many distinct values, or can be discretised into that form, for example the subdivision of the population into age groups as in the age-structured example from section
7.3
, they can be handled in the latent compartmental modelling framework by introducing extra compartments and specifying an appropriate observation model. However, covariates taking infinitely many distinct values cannot be handled this way, or necessitate further approximations.
[
58
]
have suggested methods related to PALs to construct efficient proposal distributions for SMC in individual-based models. Further research may expand the applicability of PAL-like approximations in this direction.

Michael Whitehouse is supported by a studentship from Compass – the EPSRC Centre for Doctoral
Training in Computational Statistics and Data Science. Lorenzo Rimella is supported by EP-
SRC Grant EP/R018561/1 (Bayes for Health). The authors are grateful to Nikolas Kantas for pointing them to the measles model, Patrick Cannon and colleagues at Improbable for discussion of automatic differentiation and David Greenwood for discussions of agent-based models.

Appendix A
Proofs and supporting results for section
4

Proof of Lemma
1
.

For the first result, consider the probability mass function of
{\mathbf{x}}
:

p({\mathbf{x}})=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{e^{-\lambda^{(j)}}(\lambda^{(j)})^{(x^{(j)})}}{x^{(j)}!},

and for
0\leq\bar{x}^{(j)}\leq x^{(j)}

j=1,\dots m
,

p(\bar{\mathbf{x}}\mid\mathbf{x})=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{x^{(j)}!}{\bar{x}^{(j)}!(x^{(j)}-\bar{x}^{(j)})!}(\delta^{(j)})^{\bar{x}^{(j)}}(1-\delta^{(j)})^{x^{(j)}-\bar{x}^{(j)}},

So that

p(\mathbf{x},\bar{\mathbf{x}})=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{e^{-\lambda^{(j)}}(\lambda^{(j)})^{x^{(j)}}(\delta^{(j)})^{\bar{x}^{(j)}}(1-\delta^{(j)})^{x^{(j)}-\bar{x}^{(j)}}}{\bar{x}^{(j)}!(x^{(j)}-\bar{x}^{(j)})!},

and

\displaystyle p(\bar{\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x^{(i)}\geq\bar{x}^{(i)};i\in[m]}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{e^{-\lambda^{(j)}}(\lambda^{(j)})^{x^{(j)}}(\delta^{(j)})^{\bar{x}^{(j)}}(1-\delta^{(j)})^{x^{(j)}-\bar{x}^{(j)}}}{\bar{x}^{(j)}!(x^{(j)}-\bar{x}^{(j)})!}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{e^{-\lambda^{(j)}}(\delta^{(j)}\lambda^{(j)})^{\bar{x}^{(j)}}}{\bar{x}^{(j)}!}\right)\sum_{x^{(i)}\geq\bar{x}^{(i)};i\in[m]}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{(\lambda^{(j)})^{(x^{(j)}-\bar{x}^{(j)})}(1-\delta^{(j)})^{(x^{(j)}-\bar{x}^{(j)})}}{{(x^{(j)}-\bar{x}^{(j)})!}}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{e^{-\lambda^{(j)}}(\delta^{(j)}\lambda^{(j)})^{\bar{x}^{(j)}}}{\bar{x}^{(j)}!}\right)e^{\lambda^{(j)}(1-\delta^{(j)})}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{e^{-\lambda^{(j)}\delta^{(j)}}(\delta^{(j)}\lambda^{(j)})^{\bar{x}^{(j)}}}{\bar{x}^{(j)}!},

which is the probability mass function associated with
\mathrm{Pois}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta})
.

Now consider
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\sim{M}_{t}(\bar{\mathbf{x}},\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}),\cdot)
where
\bar{\mathbf{x}}\sim\mu
, so that
\sum_{\bar{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{m}}\mu(\bar{\mathbf{x}})M_{t}(\bar{\mathbf{x}},\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}),\cdot)
is the marginal probability mass function of
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
. By the definition of
M_{t}
,
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\mathbf{Z})
, where the rows of
\mathbf{Z}
are conditionally independent given
\bar{\mathbf{x}}
, and the
i
th row of
\mathbf{Z}
is distributed
\mathrm{Mult}(\bar{x}^{(i)},\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta})}^{(i,\cdot)})
. Now we can write the moment generating function (m.g.f.) of
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{b})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{b}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\sum^{m}_{i,j=1}Z^{(i,j)}b^{(j)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{m}\exp\left(\sum^{m}_{i=1}Z^{(i,j)}b^{(j)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\sum^{m}_{j=1}Z^{(i,j)}b^{(j)}\right)\Bigg|\bar{\mathbf{x}}\right]\right\}.

Now we notice that
\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\sum^{m}_{i=1}Z^{(i,j)}b^{(i)}\right)\Bigg|\bar{\mathbf{x}}\right]
is the m.g.f. of
\mathrm{Mult}(\bar{x}^{(i)},\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta})}^{(i,\cdot)})
so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{b})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{m}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta})}^{(i,j)}e^{b^{(i)}}\right]^{\bar{x}^{(j)}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{x}^{(1)},\dots,\bar{x}^{(m)}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{m}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta})}^{(i,j)}e^{b^{(j)}}\right]^{\bar{x}^{(i)}}\frac{e^{-\lambda^{(i)}\delta^{(i)}}(\lambda^{(i)}\delta^{(i)})^{\bar{x}^{(i)}}}{\bar{x}^{(i)}!}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}e^{-\lambda^{(i)}\delta^{(i)}}\right)\sum_{\bar{x}^{(1)},\dots,\bar{x}^{(m)}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{m}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{1}{\bar{x}^{(i)}!}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}\lambda^{(i)}\delta^{(i)}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta})}^{(i,j)}e^{b^{(j)}}\right]^{\bar{x}^{(i)}}

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\exp\left(-\lambda^{(i)}\delta^{(i)}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\lambda^{(i)}\delta^{(i)}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta})}^{(i,j)}e^{b^{(j)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(-\lambda^{(i)}\delta^{i)}\sum_{j=1}^{m}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta})}^{(i,j)}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\lambda^{(i)}\delta^{(i)}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta})}^{(i,j)}e^{b^{(i)}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\exp\left\{\left((\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta})^{\top}\mathbf{K}^{(\cdot,j)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta})}\right)\left(e^{b^{(j)}}-1\right)\right\}.

We recognise this is the moment generating function of a
\mathrm{Pois}\left((\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta})^{\top}\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta})}\right)
random vector.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2
.

We have
\bar{\mathbf{y}}\sim\mathrm{Pois}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\mathbf{q})
by the same reasoning as lemma
1
. By definition
\hat{\mathbf{y}}=\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\mathbf{M}
, hence the moment generating function of
\hat{\mathbf{y}}
is:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}(\mathbf{b})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{b}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\sum^{m}_{j=1}M^{(i,j)}b^{(j)}\right)\Bigg|\bar{\mathbf{y}}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{m}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}G^{(i,j)}e^{b^{(i)}}\right]^{\bar{y}^{(j)}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{x}^{(1)},\dots,\bar{x}^{(m)}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{m}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}G^{(i,j)}e^{b^{(j)}}\right]^{\bar{x}^{(i)}}\frac{e^{-\lambda^{(i)}q^{(i)}}(\lambda^{(i)}q^{(i)})^{\bar{x}^{(i)}}}{\bar{x}^{(i)}!}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\exp\left\{\left((\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\boldsymbol{q})^{\top}\mathbf{G}^{(\cdot,j)}\right)\left(e^{b^{(j)}}-1\right)\right\}.

Which we recognise as the moment generating function of the
\mathrm{Pois}((\boldsymbol{\lambda}\odot\mathbf{q})^{\top}\mathbf{G})
, the first result of the lemma then follows from applying element-wise the fact that the intensity of the sum of two independent Poisson random variables is the sum of the intensities.

We start the proof of (
4.7
) by considering the decomposition of
\mathbf{x}
into the sum of random variables
\bar{\mathbf{y}}
and
\breve{\mathbf{x}}
where
\breve{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{y}}
. Then,
\bar{\mathbf{y}}
and
\breve{\mathbf{x}}
are independent Poisson with intensity vectors
\mathbf{q}\odot\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and
(\mathbf{1}_{m}-\mathbf{q})\odot\boldsymbol{\lambda}
respectively, see
[
40
]
[Sec. 1.2]. Since
\breve{\mathbf{x}}
is independent of
\mathbf{y}
, we have that:

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{x}\mid\mathbf{y}\right]=\left[\mathbf{1}_{m}-\mathbf{q}\right]\odot\boldsymbol{\lambda}+\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{\mathbf{y}}\mid\mathbf{y}\right].

(A.1)

So, we need to characterise the distribution of
\bar{\mathbf{y}}
given
\mathbf{y}
. Construct the random variable
\mathbf{\Xi}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{(m+1)\times m}
such that for
i,j\in[m]
,
\Xi^{(i,j)}=M^{(i,j)}
and row
m+1
of
\mathbf{\Xi}
are the counts
\hat{\mathbf{y}}\sim\mathrm{Pois}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})
. By this construction,
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\Xi^{(i,j)}=\bar{y}^{(i)}
for
i=1,\dots,m
and
\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}\Xi^{(i,j)}=y^{(j)}
for
j=1,\dots,m
. Furthermore, the elements of
\mathbf{\Xi}
are independently Poisson, see
[
40
]
[Sec. 1.2], with intensity matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{(m+1)\times m}
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(i,j)}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{(i)}q^{(i)}G^{(i,j)}\quad\mathrm{for}i=1,\dots m,\quad j=1,\dots m

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(m+1,j)}

\displaystyle=\kappa^{(j)}\quad\mathrm{for}j=1,\dots m.

If, for some
j,k\in[m]
,
\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}\Lambda^{(i,j)}=0
, then we must have that
\Lambda^{(i,j)}=0
for all
i=1,\dots,m+1
so that
\Xi^{(i,j)}=0\quad a.s.
. Otherwise we have that for
i=1,\dots,m+1
and
j\in[m]
,
\Xi^{(i,j)}
conditioned on
\sum_{k=1}^{m+1}\Xi^{(k,j)}=y^{(j)}
is distributed

\mathrm{Bin}\left(y^{(j)},\frac{\Lambda^{(i,j)}}{\sum_{k=1}^{m+1}\Lambda^{(k,j)}}\right).

Hence, given
\mathbf{y}
,
\bar{y}^{(i)}
has a Poisson-Binomial distribution with mean:

\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{y}^{(i)}\mid\mathbf{y}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}\Xi^{(i,j)}\mid\mathbf{y}\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{m}y^{(j)}\frac{\lambda^{(i)}q^{(i)}G^{(i,j)}}{\sum_{k=1}^{m+1}\lambda^{(k)}q^{(k)}G^{(k,j)}+\kappa^{(j)}},

(A.2)

for
i=1,\dots,m
, where we set the
j
th term of the outer sum on the r.h.s to
0
if
{\sum_{k=1}^{m+1}\lambda^{(k)}q^{(k)}G^{(k,j)}+\kappa^{(j)}}=0
since that achieves

\mathbb{E}\left[\Xi^{(i,j)}\mid\mathbf{y}\right]=0.

Writing (
A.2
) in vector form and substituting into (
A.1
) completes the proof.

∎

Proof of Lemma
3
.

Note
\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\mu}}[\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}])=\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda})=\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\top})
. Let
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}\sim\bar{M}_{t}(\mathbf{Z},\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),\cdot)
, then the moment generating function for
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}
is:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}(\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}\odot\mathbf{B})\mathbf{1}_{m})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{m}\exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tilde{Z}^{(i,j)}b^{(i,j)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{m}\exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tilde{Z}^{(i,j)}b^{(i,j)}\right)\bigg|\mathbf{Z}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tilde{Z}^{(i,j)}b^{(i,j)}\right)\bigg|x^{(i)}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}^{(i,j)}e^{b^{(i,j)}}\right)^{x^{(i)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}e^{-\lambda^{(i)}}\right)\sum_{(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(m)})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}^{(i,j)}e^{b^{(i,j)}}\lambda^{(i)}\right)^{x^{(i)}}\frac{1}{x^{(i)}!}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\exp\{-\lambda^{(i)}\sum_{j=1}^{m}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}^{(i,j)}(1-e^{b^{(i,j)}})\}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i,j=1}^{m}\exp\{-\lambda^{(i)}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}^{(i,j)}(1-e^{b^{(i,j)}})\},

which we recognise as the moment generating function of a
\text{Pois}((\boldsymbol{\lambda}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m})\odot\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})})
random matrix.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4
.

We have:

p(\mathbf{Z})=\prod_{i,j=1}^{m}\frac{e^{-\Lambda^{(i,j)}}(\Lambda^{(i,j)})^{Z^{(i,j)}}}{Z^{(i,j)}!},

furthermore:

p(\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{Z})=\prod_{i,j=1}^{m}\frac{Z^{(i,j)}!}{Y^{(i,j)}!(Z^{(i,j)}-Y^{(i,j)})!}{Q^{(i,j)}}^{Y^{(i,j)}}(1-Q^{(i,j)})^{Z^{(i,j)}-Y^{(i,j)}}.

So that:

p(\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Y})=\prod_{i,j=1}^{m}\frac{{Q^{(i,j)}}^{Y^{(i,j)}}(1-Q^{(i,j)})^{Z^{(i,j)}-Y^{(i,j)}}e^{-\Lambda^{(i,j)}}(\Lambda^{(i,j)})^{Z^{(i,j)}}}{Y^{(i,j)}!(Z^{(i,j)}-Y^{(i,j)})!},

and

\displaystyle p(\mathbf{Y})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{Z^{(i,j)}:Z^{(i,j)}\geq Y^{(i,j)}\}}\prod_{i,j=1}^{m}\frac{{Q^{(i,j)}}^{Y^{(i,j)}}(1-Q^{(i,j)})^{Z^{(i,j)}-Y^{(i,j)}}e^{-\Lambda^{(i,j)}}(\Lambda^{(i,j)})^{Z^{(i,j)}}}{Y^{(i,j)}!(Z^{(i,j)}-Y^{(i,j)})!}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i,j=1}^{m}\frac{e^{-\Lambda^{(i,j)}}({Q^{(i,j)}\Lambda^{(i,j)}})^{Y^{(i,j)}}}{Y^{(i,j)}!}\sum_{Z^{(i,j)}-Y^{(i,j)}\geq 0}\frac{(\Lambda^{(i,j)}(1-Q^{(i,j)}))^{Z^{(i,j)}-Y^{(i,j)}}}{(Z^{(i,j)}-Y^{(i,j)})!}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i,j=1}^{m}\frac{e^{-\Lambda^{(i,j)}}({Q^{(i,j)}\Lambda^{(i,j)}})^{Y^{(i,j)}}}{Y^{(i,j)}!}e^{\Lambda^{(i,j)}(1-Q^{(i,j)})}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i,j=1}^{m}\frac{e^{-\Lambda^{(i,j)}Q^{(i,j)}}({Q^{(i,j)}\Lambda^{(i,j)}})^{Y^{(i,j)}}}{Y^{(i,j)}!}.

Dividing
p(\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Y})
by
p(\mathbf{Y})
gives:

p(\mathbf{Z}\mid\mathbf{Y})=\prod_{i,j=1}^{m}\frac{e^{-\Lambda^{(i,j)}(1-Q^{(i,j)})}}{(Z^{(i,j)}-Y^{(i,j)})!}(\Lambda^{(i,j)}(1-Q^{(i,j)}))^{Z^{(i,j)}-Y^{(i,j)}}.

Giving the desired probability mass function of
\mathbf{Y}+\mathbf{Z}^{*}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5
.

By lemma
4
we have that for each
t=1,\ldots,\tau
,
\mathbf{Y}_{t}\sim\text{Pois}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t}\odot\mathbf{Q}_{t})
and:

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{Z}_{\tau}\mid\mathbf{Y}_{\tau}\right]=(\mathbf{1}_{m}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}-\mathbf{Q}_{\tau})\odot\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{\tau}+\mathbf{Y}_{\tau}.

Since
\bar{Y}^{(i,j)}
is the sum of independent Poisson random variables
Y_{t}^{(i,j)}
, we have
\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathrm{Pois}(\sum_{t=1}^{\tau}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t}\odot\mathbf{Q}_{t})
and given
\bar{Y}^{(i,j)}
,
Y_{\tau}^{(i,j)}
is distributed
\mathrm{Bin}(\bar{Y}^{(i,j)},\Lambda_{\tau}^{(i,j)}Q^{(i,j)}_{t}/\sum_{t=1}^{\tau}\Lambda_{t}^{(i,j)}Q^{(i,j)}_{t})
. Hence by the tower law:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{Z}_{\tau}\mid\mathbf{Y}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{Z}_{\tau}\mid\mathbf{Y}_{\tau}\right]\mid\mathbf{Y}\right]

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{m}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}-\mathbf{Q}_{t})\odot\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{\tau}+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{Y}_{\tau}\mid\mathbf{Y}\right]

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{m}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}-\mathbf{Q}_{t})\odot\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{\tau}+\bar{\mathbf{Y}}\odot\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{\tau}\odot\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}\oslash\left(\sum_{t=1}^{\tau}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t}\odot\mathbf{Q}_{t}\right),

in the case that all elements of
\sum_{t=1}^{\tau}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t}\odot\mathbf{Q}_{t}
are strictly positive. Otherwise we have

\mathbb{E}[Z^{(i,j)}_{\tau}\mid\mathbf{Y}]=(1-Q^{(i,j)}_{t})\Lambda^{(i,j)}_{\tau}
for any
(i,j)
such that
\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\tau}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t}\odot\mathbf{Q}_{t}\right]^{(i,j)}=0
, since
the latter equality implies
\left[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{\tau}\odot\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}\right]^{(i,j)}=0
, which in turn implies
Y_{\tau}^{(i,j)}=0
almost surely.

∎

Appendix B
Proofs and supporting results for section
5

B.1
Laws of Large Numbers

B.1.1
Preliminaries

Lemma 6
.

Let
\lambda,\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
X_{n}\sim\text{Pois}(\lambda_{n})
for
n=1,2,\dots
. Assume that for all
n
,
|\frac{\lambda_{n}}{n}-\lambda|<cn^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\gamma)}
for some
c>0
and
\gamma>0
, then there exist constants
b
and
\bar{\gamma}
such that:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{X_{n}}{n}-\lambda\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq bn^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\bar{\gamma})},

furthermore

\frac{X_{n}}{n}\overset{a.s.}{\rightarrow}\lambda.

Proof.

By recurrence relations for the central moments of Poisson random variables, see e.g
[
36
]
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{X_{n}}{n}-\frac{\lambda_{n}}{n}\right|^{4}\right]

\displaystyle=n^{-4}\lambda_{n}\sum_{k=0}^{2}\binom{3}{k}\mathbb{E}\left[\left({X_{n}}-{\lambda_{n}}\right)^{k}\right]

(B.1)

\displaystyle=n^{-4}\lambda_{n}\left\{1+0+\lambda_{n}\frac{3!}{2!1!}\right\}

(B.2)

\displaystyle=n^{-2}\left\{n^{-2}\lambda_{n}+3\left(\frac{\lambda_{n}}{n}\right)^{2}\right\}\leq an^{-2},

(B.3)

for some
a>0
since the curly bracketed term in (
B.3
) defines a convergent sequence. Hence, by the Minkowski inequality:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{X_{n}}{n}-\lambda\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{X_{n}}{n}-\frac{\lambda_{n}}{n}\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}+\left|\frac{\lambda_{n}}{n}-\lambda\right|

\displaystyle\leq a^{\frac{1}{4}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}+cn^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq b\max\left(n^{-\frac{1}{2}},n^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\gamma)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq bn^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\bar{\gamma})},

where b =
a^{\frac{1}{4}}+c
and
\bar{\gamma}=\min\left(\gamma,\frac{1}{4}\right)
. Now let
\varepsilon>0
, by Markov’s inequality:

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{X_{n}}{n}-\lambda\right|>\varepsilon\right)\leq\varepsilon^{-4}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{X_{n}}{n}-\lambda\right|^{4}\right]\leq\epsilon^{-4}b^{4}n^{-(1+\bar{4}\gamma)},

So that:

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{X_{n}}{n}-\lambda\right|>\varepsilon\right)\leq\epsilon^{-4}b^{4}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-(1+4\bar{\gamma})}<\infty.

Then
n^{-1}X_{n}\rightarrow\lambda
almost surely by the Borel-Cantelli lemma.
∎

Corollary 1
.

If
\mathbf{x}_{n}\sim\mathrm{Pois}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n})
for a sequence
(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n})_{n\geq 1}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that there exists
c>0
and
\gamma>0
such that
\|n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{\infty}<cn^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\gamma)}
for some
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, then for any vector
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
there exists constants
b>0
and
\bar{\gamma}>0
such that:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{x}_{n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq bn^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\bar{\gamma})}.

Proof.

Apply lemma
6
in an element-wise fashion.
∎

Lemma 7
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a filtration and
\Delta^{(i)}
for
i=1,2,\dots
be random variables which are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}
, are bounded by a constant
|\Delta^{(i)}|\leq M<\infty
almost surely, and satisfy
\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta^{(i)}\mid\mathcal{F}\right]=0
. Let
a_{n}
be a non-negative integer valued random variable such that
\sigma(a_{n})\subseteq\mathcal{F}
and assume there exist constants
a>0
,
b>0
, and
\gamma>0
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{a_{n}}{n}-a\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq bn^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\gamma)}.

Then there exists a constant
d>0
such that:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{a_{n}}\Delta^{(i)}\right|^{4}\right]\leq dn^{-2}.

Proof.

Recalling that a sum over an empty set is equal to zero by convention, we have that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{a_{n}}\Delta^{(i)}\right)^{4}\Big|\mathcal{F}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\{k_{1}+\dots+k_{a_{n}}=4;k_{i}\geq 0\}}\binom{4}{k_{1},\dots,k_{a_{n}}}\prod_{i=1}^{a_{n}}\left(\Delta^{(i)}\right)^{k_{i}}\Big|\mathcal{F}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{k_{1}+\dots+k_{a_{n}}=4;k_{i}\geq 0\}}\binom{4}{k_{1},\dots,k_{a_{n}}}\prod_{i=1}^{a_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\Delta^{(i)}\right)^{k_{i}}\Big|\mathcal{F}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{a_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\Delta^{(i)}\right)^{4}\Big|\mathcal{F}\right]+6\sum_{\{(i,j)\in[a_{n}]^{2};i\neq j\}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\Delta^{(i)}\right)^{2}\Big|\mathcal{F}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\Delta^{(j)}\right)^{2}\Big|\mathcal{F}\right]

\displaystyle\leq a_{n}M^{4}+3a_{n}(a_{n}-1)M^{4}

\displaystyle\leq ca_{n}^{2},

for some constant
c>0
. The first equality holds by the multinomial theorem. The second equality holds through conditional independence of the
\Delta^{(i)}
. The third equality comes from the fact that all terms of the sum where
k_{i}=1
for some
i
disappear since
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\Delta^{(i)}\right)^{1}\Big|\mathcal{F}\right]=0
; hence, we need only count the terms with exclusively even
k_{i}
’s. The first term after the inequality arises since there are
a_{n}
terms with a
4
th power, each of which we can bound
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\Delta^{(j)}\right)^{4}\Big|\mathcal{F}\right]<M^{4}
. The term
3a_{n}(a_{n}-1)
comes from counting the number of terms with exactly 2 of the
k_{i}
’s equal to
2
with the rest equalling
0
; there are
\binom{a_{n}}{2}=a_{n}(a_{n}-1)/2
such pairs, multiplying this by
\binom{4}{k_{1},\dots,k_{a_{n}}}=\binom{4}{2,2,0,\dots}=6
gives a total of
3a_{n}(a_{n}-1)
, then we bound each of the
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\Delta^{(i)}\right)^{2}\Big|\mathcal{F}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\Delta^{(j)}\right)^{2}\Big|\mathcal{F}\right]\leq M^{2}M^{2}=M^{4}
for all
(i,j)\in[a_{n}]^{2}
. So we have:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{a_{n}}\Delta^{(i)}\right|^{4}\right|\mathcal{F}\right]\leq c\left(\frac{a_{n}}{n}\right)^{2}n^{-2},

(B.4)

by the Lyapunov inequality:

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\frac{a_{n}}{n}\bigg|^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}&\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\frac{a_{n}}{n}-a+a\bigg|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\\
&\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\frac{a_{n}}{n}-a\bigg|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}+a\\
&\leq bn^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\gamma)}+a\\
&\leq b+a<\infty.\end{split}

(B.5)

We now apply a tower law argument to the above to see that, for constant
{d=c\left(b+a\right)^{2}}
:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{a_{n}}\Delta^{(i)}\right|^{4}\right]\leq dn^{-2}.

(B.6)

∎

Lemma 8
.

Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{\geq 0}
,
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
c>0
, and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then:

\left|\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}-\frac{n^{-1}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}}{c}\right|\leq\left|\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|c^{-1}\left|n^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\mathbf{x}-c\right|

Proof.

If
\mathbf{x}=\boldsymbol{0}
then the result is trivial. Now, for
\mathbf{x}\neq\boldsymbol{0}
we have:

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}-\frac{n^{-1}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}}{c}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\mathbf{x}^{\top}}{\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\mathbf{x}}\boldsymbol{f}-\frac{n^{-1}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}}{c}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|\left|\frac{1}{\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\mathbf{x}}-\frac{n^{-1}}{c}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|\left|\frac{c-n^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\mathbf{x}}{c\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\mathbf{x}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{\mathbf{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}}{\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\mathbf{x}}\right|c^{-1}\left|n^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\mathbf{x}-c\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|c^{-1}\left|n^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\mathbf{x}-c\right|.

∎

B.1.2
Case (I)

Define the sequence of vectors:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nu}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})

\displaystyle:={\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{0,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})

\displaystyle:=\left[\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right)^{\top}\mathbf{K}_{t+1,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}))}\right]^{\top}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{t+1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}).

Lemma 9
.

Let assumptions
2
-
4
hold. For all
t\geq 0
there exists
\gamma_{t}>0
and for all
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
there exists
c_{t}>0
such that:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq c_{t}n^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\gamma_{t})}.

(B.7)

Proof.

Explicit dependence of some quantities on
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
and
n
is omitted throughout the proof to avoid over-cumbersome notation where the dependence is unambiguous. We proceed to prove the above by induction on
t
. At time
0
we have for some
c_{0}>0
and
\gamma_{0}>0
:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{\mathbf{x}_{0}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-\boldsymbol{\nu}_{0}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq c_{0}n^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\gamma_{0})},

(B.8)

by assumption
4
. Now for
t\geq 1
assume (
B.7
) holds for
t-1
. Recall
\mathbf{x}_{t}=\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{t}+\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}
,
\tilde{x}^{(j)}_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t-1}}\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}\mathbb{I}\{\xi_{t}^{(i)}=j\}
and
\bar{x}_{t-1}^{(j)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t-1}}\mathbb{I}[\xi_{t-1}^{(i)}=j]\mathbb{I}[\phi^{(i)}_{t}=1]
. We make the following decomposition:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-\left[\frac{\mathbf{\bar{x}}_{t-1}}{n}^{\top}\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{t,\infty}^{\top}\right]\boldsymbol{f}

(B.9)

\displaystyle+\left[\frac{\mathbf{\bar{x}}_{t-1}}{n}-\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}\right]^{\top}\left[\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})}\boldsymbol{f}\right]

(B.10)

\displaystyle+(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})^{\top}\left[\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})}-\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})}\right]\boldsymbol{f}.

(B.11)

Consider (
B.9
). We make the further decomposition:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}

\displaystyle-\left[\frac{\mathbf{\bar{x}}_{t-1}}{n}^{\top}\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{t,n}^{\top}\right]\boldsymbol{f}

(B.12)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{t}+\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{t})^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-\left[\frac{\mathbf{\bar{x}}_{t-1}}{n}^{\top}\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{t,n}^{\top}\right]\boldsymbol{f}

(B.13)

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-\left[\frac{\mathbf{\bar{x}}_{t-1}}{n}^{\top}\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})}\right]\boldsymbol{f}+\left[\frac{\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}_{t}}{n}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{t,n}^{\top}\right]\boldsymbol{f}

(B.14)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t-1}}\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}\mathbb{I}\{\xi_{t}^{(i)}=j\}\right)f^{(j)}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t-1}}\mathbb{I}\{\xi_{t-1}^{(i)}=j\}\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}\right)\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})}^{j,\cdot}\boldsymbol{f}

(B.15)

\displaystyle+\left[\frac{\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}_{t}}{n}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{t,n}^{\top}\right]\boldsymbol{f}.

(B.16)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t-1}}\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}\mathbb{I}\{\xi_{t}^{(i)}=j\}\right)f^{(j)}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t-1}}\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})}^{\xi_{t-1}^{(i)},\cdot}\boldsymbol{f}+\left[\frac{\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}_{t}}{n}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{t,n}^{\top}\right]\boldsymbol{f}.

(B.17)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t-1}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\{\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}\mathbb{I}\{\xi_{t}^{(i)}=j\}-\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})}^{(\xi_{t-1}^{(i)}),j}\right\}f^{(j)}

(B.18)

\displaystyle+\left[\frac{\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}_{t}}{n}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{t,n}^{\top}\right]\boldsymbol{f}.

(B.19)

The term
\left[\frac{\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}_{t}}{n}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{t,n}^{\top}\right]\boldsymbol{f}
converges to
0
in
L^{4}
by assumption
2
and lemma
6
, that is there exists an
\hat{c}_{t}>0
and
\hat{\gamma}_{t}>0
such that:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\left[\frac{\hat{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}_{t}}{n}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{t,n}^{\top}\right]\boldsymbol{f}\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq\hat{c}_{t}n^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\hat{\gamma}_{t})}.

(B.20)

Now, turning to (
B.18
), let
\mathcal{G}_{t}:=\sigma(\{\xi_{t}^{(i)}\}_{i=1,\dots,n_{t}})
and
\mathcal{F}_{t}:=\sigma(\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}\}_{i=1,\dots,n_{t}})
. See that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\{\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}\mathbb{I}\{\xi_{t}^{(i)}=j\}-\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{\bar{x}}_{t-1})}^{(\xi_{t-1}^{(i)}),j}\right\}f^{(j)}\Big|\mathcal{G}_{t-1}\vee\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]

(B.21)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}\mathbb{I}\{\xi_{t}^{(i)}=j\}\Big|\mathcal{G}_{t-1}\vee\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]-\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{\bar{x}}_{t-1})}^{(\xi_{t-1}^{(i)}),j}\right\}f^{(j)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\{\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{\bar{x}}_{t-1})}^{(\xi_{t-1}^{(i)}),j}-\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{\bar{x}}_{t-1})}^{(\xi_{t-1}^{(i)}),j}\right\}f^{(j)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

since, given
\xi_{t-1}^{(i)}
,
\phi_{t}^{(i)}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}\left(\delta_{t}^{(\xi_{t-1}^{(i)})}\right)
and, conditional on
\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1
and
\mathcal{G}_{t-1}
,
\xi^{(i)}_{t}
is a draw from the
\xi^{(i)}_{t-1}
th row of
\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{x}_{t-1})}
; and if
\phi_{t}^{(i)}=0
then
\xi_{t}^{(i)}=0
. Moreover:

\bigg|\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\{\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}\mathbb{I}\{\xi_{t}^{(i)}=j\}-\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})}^{(\xi_{t-1}^{(i)}),j}\right\}f^{(j)}\bigg|\leq m\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\infty}=:M.

(B.22)

Define:

\Delta_{t}^{(i)}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\{\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}\mathbb{I}\{\xi_{t}^{(i)}=j\}-\mathbb{I}\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\}K_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})}^{(\xi_{t-1}^{(i)}),j}\right\}f^{(j)}.

The
\Delta_{t}^{(i)}
are conditionally independent and mean zero given
\mathcal{G}_{t-1}\vee\mathcal{F}_{t}
, and
\sigma(n_{t-1})\subset\mathcal{G}_{t-1}\vee\mathcal{F}_{t}
. Also note that, since
\frac{n_{t-1}}{n}
is equal to
\frac{\mathbf{x}_{t-1}^{\top}}{n}\mathbf{1}_{m}
, we can invoke the induction hypothesis with test vector
\mathbf{1}_{m}
to see there exist constants
c_{t-1}
and
\gamma_{t-1}
such that:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{n_{t-1}}{n}-\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq c_{t-1}n^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\gamma_{t-1})}

so that we satisfy the conditions of lemma
7
. Hence there exists a constant
\tilde{c}_{t}>0
:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t-1}}\Delta_{t}^{(i)}\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq\tilde{c}_{t}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(B.23)

Before analysing (
B.10
) and (
B.11
) we will prove an intermediary result. Consider the decomposition:

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}}{n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}-(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg|\frac{\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}}{n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}-\left(\frac{{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}}{n}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\bigg|

(B.24)

\displaystyle+\bigg|\left(\frac{{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}}{n}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}-(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\bigg|.

(B.25)

The term in (
B.25
) converges to 0 in
L^{4}
at the required rate by the induction hypothesis with test vector
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}\odot\boldsymbol{f}
. Now for (
B.24
) see that:

\frac{\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}}{n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}-\left(\frac{{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}}{n}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t-1}}\underbrace{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{I}\left\{\xi_{t-1}^{(i)}=j\right\}\left(\mathbb{I}\left\{\phi_{t}^{(i)}=1\right\}-\delta_{t}^{(\xi_{t-1}^{(i)})}\right)f^{(j)}}_{\bar{\Delta}_{t}^{(i)}},

(B.26)

and note that the
\bar{\Delta}_{t}^{(i)}
are mean
0
, bounded, and independent given
\mathcal{G}_{t-1}
so that by lemma
7
we have that (
B.26
) converges to
0
in
L^{4}
at the required rate. Combining this with the Minkowski inequality, we have that for some positive constants
\bar{c}_{t}
and
\bar{\gamma}_{t}
:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}}{n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}-(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq\bar{c}_{t}n^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\bar{\gamma})}.

(B.27)

Now we look at (
B.10
):

\displaystyle\left|\left[\frac{\mathbf{\bar{x}}_{t-1}}{n}-\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}\right]^{\top}\left[\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})}\boldsymbol{f}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\frac{\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}}{n}-\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\right\|_{1}\left\|\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})}\boldsymbol{f}\right\|_{\infty}

(B.28)

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left|\frac{\bar{x}_{t-1}^{(i)}}{n}-\nu_{t-1}^{(i)}\delta_{t}^{(i)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left|\frac{\mathbf{\bar{x}}_{t-1}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}-(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\right|.

The first inequality here uses Holder’s inequality and the second uses the fact that the row sums of the matrix
\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})}
are equal to
1
. By (
B.27
) with
\boldsymbol{f}=\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
in conjunction with the Minkowski inequality there exists
\breve{c}_{t}>0
and
\breve{\gamma}_{t}>0
such that:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\left[\frac{\mathbf{\bar{x}}_{t-1}}{n}-\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}\right]^{\top}\left[\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})}\boldsymbol{f}\right]\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq\breve{c}_{t}n^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\breve{\gamma}_{t})}.

(B.29)

Now looking at (
B.11
) we see using assumption
3
that there exists a
c>0
such that:

\displaystyle\left|\left[\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}\right]^{\top}\left[\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})}-\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})}\right]\boldsymbol{f}\right|

\displaystyle\leq c\|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}\|_{\infty}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\infty}\|\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})-\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq c\|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}\|_{\infty}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left|(\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1})-\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}))^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\right|

If
\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})=0
then
\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}=0

\mathbb{P}^{\theta^{*}}-a.s.
by lemmas
13
and
15
, which we state and prove in section
B.2
, in which case the right hand side of the above is
0
and therefore satisfies all positive bounds. Henceforth, assume
\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})>0
. Consider:

\displaystyle\bigg|\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{\bar{x}_{t-1}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}

\displaystyle-\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\bigg|

(B.30)

\displaystyle=\bigg|\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{\bar{x}_{t-1}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}-\frac{(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}}{\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})}\bigg|

(B.31)

\displaystyle=\bigg|\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{\bar{x}_{t-1}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}+\frac{n^{-1}\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}^{\top}}{\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})}\boldsymbol{f}-\frac{n^{-1}\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}^{\top}}{\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})}\boldsymbol{f}-\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\bigg|

(B.32)

\displaystyle\leq\bigg|\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{\bar{x}_{t-1}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}-\frac{n^{-1}\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}^{\top}}{\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})}\boldsymbol{f}\bigg|+\bigg|\frac{n^{-1}\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}^{\top}}{\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})}\boldsymbol{f}-\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\bigg|

(B.33)

\displaystyle\leq\left|\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{\bar{x}_{t-1}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}))^{-1}\left|\frac{\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}}{n}-\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})\right|

(B.34)

\displaystyle+(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}))^{-1}\left|\frac{\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|

(B.35)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{m\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\infty}}{\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})}\left|\frac{\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}}{n}-\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})\right|

(B.36)

\displaystyle+(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}))^{-1}\left|\frac{\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{t-1}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|.

(B.37)

Where we use lemma
8
in line (
B.34
). We can again invoke (
B.27
) to give
L^{4}
convergence of (
B.36
) and (
B.37
) at the required rate. We can now combine all of the above, along with the Minkowski inequality to show that:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq c_{t}n^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\gamma_{t})},

(B.38)

where
c_{t}=\hat{c}_{t}+\tilde{c}_{t}+\bar{c}_{t}+\breve{c}_{t}
, and
\gamma_{t}=\min(\hat{\gamma}_{t},\frac{1}{4},\bar{\gamma}_{t},\breve{\gamma}_{t})
.
∎

Lemma 10
.

Let assumptions
2
-
4
hold. Then there exists a constant
\rho_{t}>0
for each
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
t\geq 1
, and a constant
a_{t}>0
such that:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{\mathbf{y}^{\top}_{t}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-\left([\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\odot\mathbf{q}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})]^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})+\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})^{\top}\right)\boldsymbol{f}\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq a_{t}n^{-(\frac{1}{4}+{\rho}_{t})}.

Proof.

Explicit dependence of some quantities on
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
and
n
is omitted throughout the proof to avoid over-cumbersome notation where the dependence is unambiguous. First note that:

\frac{\mathbf{y}_{t}}{n}=\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t}}{n}+\frac{\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{t}}{n},

(B.39)

and

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{\top}_{t}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{t,\infty}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}<\hat{a}_{t}n^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\hat{\rho}_{t})},

(B.40)

for some
\hat{a}_{t}>0
and
\hat{\rho}_{t}>0
by corollary
1
and assumption
2
. Write:

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{\top}_{t}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t})^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}\boldsymbol{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{\top}_{t}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-\left(\frac{{\mathbf{x}}_{t}}{n}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}\boldsymbol{f}

(B.41)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{{\mathbf{x}}_{t}}{n}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}\boldsymbol{f}-\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}\boldsymbol{f}.

(B.42)

We have that

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\left(\frac{{\mathbf{x}}_{t}}{n}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}\boldsymbol{f}-\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}\boldsymbol{f}\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq\bar{a}_{t}n^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\bar{\rho}_{t})}

(B.43)

for some
\bar{a}_{t}>0
and
\bar{\rho}_{t}>0
by lemma
9
using test function
[\left(\mathbf{q}_{t}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}\right)\odot\mathbf{G}_{t}]\boldsymbol{f}
.

Furthermore, we have:

\begin{split}\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\mathbf{f}&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(j)}\boldsymbol{f}^{(j)}\\
&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\mathbb{I}\{\xi_{t}^{(i)}=k\}\mathbb{I}\{\zeta_{t}^{(i)}=1\}\mathbb{I}\{\varsigma_{t}^{(i)}=j\}\boldsymbol{f}^{(j)},\end{split}

(B.44)

where
\zeta_{t}^{(i)}\sim\text{Bernoulli}(\mathbf{q}_{t}^{(\xi_{t}^{(i)})})
and
\varsigma_{t}^{(i)}\sim\text{Categorical}(\mathbf{G}^{(\xi_{t}^{(i)},\cdot)})
indicates the compartment in which it is observed.
Notice that for (
B.41
) :

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{\top}_{t}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-

\displaystyle\left(\frac{{\mathbf{x}}_{t}}{n}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}\boldsymbol{f}

(B.45)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t}}\underbrace{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\xi_{t}^{(i)}=k\}\mathbb{I}\{\zeta_{t}^{(i)}=1\}\mathbb{I}\{\varsigma_{t}^{(i)}=j\}-\mathbb{I}\{\xi_{t}^{(i)}=k\}q_{t}^{(k)}G_{t}^{(k,j)}\right]\boldsymbol{f}^{(j)}}_{=:\Xi_{t}^{(i)}}.

The
\Xi_{t}^{(i)}
are mean zero and independent conditioned on
\mathcal{G}_{t}
. Furthermore:

|\Xi_{t}^{(i)}|\leq m^{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\infty},

(B.46)

almost surely and
\sigma(n_{t})\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{t}
where
\mathcal{G}_{t}
is defined as in lemma
9
. An application of lemma
7
, yields:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{\top}_{t}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-\left(\frac{{\mathbf{x}}_{t}}{n}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}\boldsymbol{f}\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq\tilde{a}_{t}n^{(\frac{1}{4}+\tilde{\rho}_{t})},

(B.47)

for some constants
\tilde{a}_{t}>0
and
\tilde{\rho}_{t}>0
. Combining (
B.40
), (
B.43
), and (
B.47
) with the Minkowski inequality yields:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{\mathbf{y}^{\top}_{t}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-\left((\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t})^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}+\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{t}^{\top}\right)\boldsymbol{f}\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq a_{t}n^{-(\frac{1}{4}+{\rho}_{t})},

(B.48)

where
a_{t}=\hat{a}_{t}+\bar{a}_{t}+\tilde{a}_{t}
and
\rho_{t}=\min(\hat{\rho}_{t},\bar{\rho}_{t},\tilde{\rho}_{t})
.

∎

Proposition 1
.

Let assumptions
2
-
4
hold. Then for all
t\geq 1
:

\frac{\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}[\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\odot\mathbf{q}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})]^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})+\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})^{\top}.

(B.49)

Proof.

By lemma
10
there exists constants
a_{t}>0
and
\rho_{t}>0
such that:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-((\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t})^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}+\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{t})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|^{4}\right]\leq a_{t}^{4}n^{-(1+4\rho_{t})}.

By Markov’s inequality:

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}\left[\left|\frac{\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-((\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t})^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}+\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{t}^{\top})\boldsymbol{f}\right|>\varepsilon\right]&\leq\varepsilon^{-4}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-((\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t})^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}+\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{t}^{\top})\boldsymbol{f}\right|^{4}\right]\\
&\leq\varepsilon^{-4}a_{t}^{4}n^{-(1+4\rho_{t})}.\end{split}

This implies that:

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}\left[\left|\frac{\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-((\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t})^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}+\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{t}^{\top})\boldsymbol{f}\right|>\varepsilon\right]<\infty.

(B.50)

We now appeal to the Borel-Cantelli lemma which tells us that (
B.50
) implies the event:

\left\{\left|\frac{\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-((\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t})^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}+\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{t}^{\top})\boldsymbol{f}\right|>\varepsilon\right\},

happens for infinitely many
n
with probability 0, and that:

\mathbb{P}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}\left(\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left|\frac{\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}-((\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t})^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}+\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{t}^{\top})\boldsymbol{f}\right|>\varepsilon\right)=0,

for all
\varepsilon>0
. Hence we have shown that:

\frac{\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\boldsymbol{f}\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}((\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t})^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}+\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{t}^{\top})\boldsymbol{f}.

∎

B.1.3
Case (II)

Define:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nu}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{0,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}\right)\odot\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\mathbf{N}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}))^{\top}.

Lemma 11
.

Let assumptions
2
-
4
hold. For all
t\geq 1
there exists a
\gamma_{t_{z}}>0
, and for all vectors
\boldsymbol{f}_{1},\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
a constant
b_{t}>0
, such that:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|n^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}_{t}\boldsymbol{f}_{2}-\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{N}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\right|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq b_{t}n^{-(\frac{1}{4}+\gamma_{t_{z}})},\text{ for all }t\geq 0.

Proof.

Recall from section
4.2
that in case (II) there is no immigration or emigration,
n_{t}=n
and hence also
\mathbf{x}_{t}=\mathbf{\bar{x}}_{t}
with probability
1
for all
t\geq 0
.

Consider the decomposition:

\displaystyle\big|n^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}_{t}\boldsymbol{f}_{2}

\displaystyle-\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\left[\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}\right]\odot\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1})}\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\big|

(B.51)

\displaystyle\leq\left|n^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}_{t}\boldsymbol{f}_{2}-\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\left[\frac{\mathbf{x}_{t-1}}{n}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}\right]\odot\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1})}\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\right|

(B.52)

\displaystyle+\left|\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\left[\frac{\mathbf{x}_{t-1}}{n}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}\right]\odot\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1})}\boldsymbol{f}_{2}-\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\left[\frac{\mathbf{x}_{t-1}}{n}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}\right]\odot\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1})}\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\right|

(B.53)

\displaystyle+\left|\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\left[\frac{\mathbf{x}_{t-1}}{n}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}\right]\odot\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1})}\boldsymbol{f}_{2}-\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\left[\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}\right]\odot\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{t-1})}\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\right|.

(B.54)

Notice that by assumption
2
with vectors
\boldsymbol{f}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{f}_{2}
, there exists a constant
c>0
such that the term (
B.53
) satisfies:
