\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{\tau_{1}}\Lambda^{(i,j)}_{s,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot Q^{(i,j)}_{s}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=0.

This completes the case
r=1
.

Now assume that for all
s=\tau_{r-1}+1,\dots,\tau_{r}
,
\Lambda_{s,\infty}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
, which implies
\Lambda_{s,\infty}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})=0
and that
M^{(i,j)}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
, which implies
M^{(i,j)}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})=0
. Then

\displaystyle\bar{\Lambda}^{(i,j)}_{\tau_{r},\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\left[1-Q^{(i,j)}_{\tau_{r}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]\odot\Lambda^{(i,j)}_{\tau_{r}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle+\frac{M^{(i,j)}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})}{M^{(i,j)}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})}\odot\left[\Lambda^{(i,j)}_{\tau_{r},\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot Q^{(i,j)}_{\tau_{r}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]=0,

and either:

•

Q^{(i,j)}_{\tau_{r}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})<1
, which implies
\Lambda^{(i,j)}_{\tau_{r},\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})=0\implies\Lambda^{(i,j)}_{\tau_{r},n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\implies\bar{\Lambda}^{(i,j)}_{\tau_{r},n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
a.s., so that the first term is
0
; or

•

Q^{(i,j)}_{\tau_{r}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=1
which implies
\Lambda^{(i,j)}_{\tau_{r},\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})=0\implies\Lambda^{(i,j)}_{\tau_{r},n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\implies\bar{\Lambda}^{(i,j)}_{\tau_{r},n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
, a.s., so that the right hand term is
0
.

This along with using the same reasoning used in the
r=1
case tells us that given:

\Lambda_{\tau_{r},\infty}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})=\left(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\bar{\Lambda}}_{\tau_{r}-1,\infty}^{(\cdot,i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)K^{(i,j)}_{1,\boldsymbol{\eta}\left(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\bar{\Lambda}}_{\tau_{r}-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=0,

which implies

\Lambda_{\tau_{r},n}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\left(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\bar{\Lambda}}_{\tau_{r}-1,n}^{(\cdot,i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)K^{(i,j)}_{1,\boldsymbol{\eta}\left(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\bar{\Lambda}}_{\tau_{r}-1,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=0\quad a.s.

Using this and further using identical inductive reasoning to the
r=1
case we see that for all
s\in\{\tau_{r}+1,\dots,\tau_{r+1}\}
we have
\Lambda_{s,\infty}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})=0\implies\Lambda_{s,n}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
a.s. and further that
M^{(i,j)}_{r+1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
, which implies
M^{(i,j)}_{r+1,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
a.s. This completes the inductive proof.
∎

Lemma 20
.

Let assumptions
2
-
4
hold. For all
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\in\Theta
and
r\geq 1
, the function
\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
is continuous on
\Theta
.

Proof.

The arguments are very similar to those in the proof of
16
, but making use of lemma
18
, so we omit them.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4
.

The proof is by induction on
r
. Consider
r=1
. Note that
n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\bar{\lambda}}_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{0,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
by assumption
4
.
Now let
t=1,\dots,\tau_{1}-1
and assume that
n^{-1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t-1,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})
. Then:

\displaystyle n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=n^{-1}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t-1,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m})\odot\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t-1,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)}

\displaystyle\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}\right)\odot\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Lambda}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}).

By the CMT, a further application yields:

n^{-1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=n^{-1}(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}).

Then by induction on
t
we have that:

\displaystyle n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}),

for all
t=1,\dots,\tau_{1}
, this means that:

\displaystyle n^{-1}\mathbf{M}_{1,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=n^{-1}

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{\tau_{1}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\mathbf{Q}_{s}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{\tau_{1}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\mathbf{Q}_{s}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathbf{M}_{1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}).

Now for general
r=1,\dots,R
assume that
\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{\tau_{r-1},n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{\tau_{r-1},\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
. Using identical reasoning to the
r=1
case, we find that for all
t=\tau_{r-1}+1,\dots,\tau_{r}
:

\displaystyle n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}),

which in turn implies by the CMT that:

\displaystyle n^{-1}\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}),

Writing out the definition of
M_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})
, proposition
2
gives
n^{-1}\bar{\mathbf{Y}}_{{r}}\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})
. Then by the CMT,

\displaystyle n^{-1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{\tau_{r},n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{1}_{m}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}-\mathbf{Q}_{\tau_{r}}^{*}\right)\odot n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{\tau_{r},n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle+\left[n^{-1}\bar{\mathbf{Y}}_{{r}}\oslash n^{-1}\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]\odot\left(\left[n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{\tau_{r},n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\mathbf{Q}_{\tau_{r}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]\right)

\displaystyle\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\left(\mathbf{1}_{m}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}-\mathbf{Q}_{\tau_{r}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)\odot\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\tau_{r},\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle+\left[\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\oslash\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]\odot\left[\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\tau_{r},\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\mathbf{Q}_{\tau_{r}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbf{\Lambda}}_{\tau_{r},\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}).

We note here that the left hand side of the above display is almost surely well defined since, by lemma
17
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i,j\in[m]
if there is positive probability that
M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
then
\bar{Y}^{(i,j)}_{{r}}=0

\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}
-a.s., in which case we invoke the convention
\frac{0}{0}\coloneqq 0
. The right hand side of the limit is well defined since for all
i,j\in[m]
we have
M^{(i,j)}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=0\iff M^{(i,j)}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
by lemma
18
, in which case we again invoke the convection
\frac{0}{0}\coloneqq 0
. A further application of the CMT gives:

n^{-1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{\tau_{r},n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=n^{-1}\left(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{\tau_{r}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)^{\top}\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}(\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\bar{\mathbf{\Lambda}}_{\tau_{r},\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{\tau_{r},\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}).

This completes the proof.
∎

B.3
Contrast functions

Definition 1
.

Let
(\mathcal{H}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of random functions
\mathcal{H}_{n}:\theta\in\Theta\mapsto\mathcal{H}_{n}(\theta)\in\mathbb{R}
where
\Theta
is a metric space. We say that
(\mathcal{H}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
are stochastically equicontinuous if there exists an event
M
of probability
1
, such that for all
\varepsilon>0
and
\omega\in M
, there exists
N(\omega)
and
\delta>0
such that
n>N(\omega)
implies:

\sup_{|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}|<\delta}|\mathcal{H}_{n}(\omega,\theta_{1})-\mathcal{H}_{n}(\omega,\theta_{2})|<\epsilon.

Lemma 21
.

Assume
\Theta
is a compact metric space and let
(\mathcal{H}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of random functions
\mathcal{H}_{n}:\theta\in\Theta\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{n}(\theta)\in\mathbb{R}
. If there exists a continuous function
\mathcal{H}
such that for all
\theta\in\Theta
we have
|\mathcal{H}_{n}(\theta)-\mathcal{H}(\theta)|\overset{a.s.}{\rightarrow}0
, and
(\mathcal{H}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
are stochastically equicontinuous, then:

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}|\mathcal{H}_{n}(\theta)-\mathcal{H}(\theta)|\overset{a.s.}{\rightarrow}0.

That is
\mathcal{H}_{n}(\theta)
converges to
\mathcal{H}(\theta)
almost surely as
n\rightarrow\infty
, uniformly in
\theta
.

Proof.

See
[
4
]
.
∎

B.3.1
Case (I)

We have:

\displaystyle n^{-1}\ell_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-n^{-1}\ell_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Bigg\{

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\log\big(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\oslash\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\big)

(B.90)

\displaystyle-n^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\left[\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right]\Bigg\}.

The following proposition details the limit of (
B.90
).

Proposition 5
.

Let assumptions
1
-
4
hold. Then:

n^{-1}\ell_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-n^{-1}\ell_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{KL}\left(\mathrm{Pois}\left[\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right]\|\mathrm{Pois}\left[\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]\right),

(B.91)

uniformly in
\boldsymbol{\theta}
.

Proof.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define a random function
\mathcal{C}_{n}:\Theta\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, as
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\coloneqq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathcal{C}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
where:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\log\big(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\oslash\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\big)-n^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\left[\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{y_{t}^{(i)}}{n}\log\frac{\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}{\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})}-n^{-1}\left[\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right],

with the convention
0\log 0\coloneqq 0
. To see that
\mathcal{C}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is almost surely well defined, consider the following cases for each
i\in[m]
. If both
\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})>0
and
\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})>0
,
\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}
-a.s., then the
\log
of the ratio of these terms is almost surely well defined. If
\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
or
\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=0
with positive probability, then
y^{(i)}_{t}=0

\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}
-a.s. by lemma
13
and we invoke the convention
0\log 0\coloneqq 0
.

We shall show that for
t=1,\dots,T
,

\mathcal{C}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}-\mathrm{KL}(\mathrm{Pois}[\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})]\|\mathrm{Pois}[\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})]),\text{ uniformly in }\boldsymbol{\theta}.

The proof consists of showing pointwise convergence and then stochastic equicontinuity of
\mathcal{C}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
. Uniform almost sure convergence then follows by lemma
21
.

Fix
t\in\{1,\dots,T\}
and note that
\frac{\mathbf{y}_{t}}{n}\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})
by proposition
1
(see remark
1
), and by proposition
3
,

n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
. We claim that by the CMT:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}}{n}\log\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\oslash\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right)-n^{-1}\left[\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right]^{\top}\mathbf{1}_{m}

(B.92)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{y_{t}^{(i)}}{n}\log\frac{\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}{\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})}-n^{-1}\left[\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right]

(B.93)

\displaystyle\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{t,\infty}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\log\frac{\mu_{t,\infty}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})}{\mu_{t,\infty}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})}-\left[\mu_{t,\infty}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mu_{t,\infty}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right]

(B.94)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{KL}\left(\text{Pois}\left[\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})]\right)\|\text{Pois}\left[\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]\right).

(B.95)

To see that the limit is well defined consider the cases for each
i\in[m]
, either:

•

\mu_{t,\infty}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})>0
and
\mu_{t,\infty}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})>0.
In this case all functions in the sequence
\{\mathcal{C}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\}_{n\geq 1}
and its limit are well defined; or

•

\mu_{t,\infty}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})>0
and
\mu_{t,\infty}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=0
, or
\mu_{t,\infty}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
and
\mu_{t,\infty}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})>0
. This case is prohibited by lemma
14
; or

•

\mu_{t,\infty}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mu_{t,\infty}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=0
. In this case, by lemmas
13
and
15
, we have that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}

\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=0
,
\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
, and
y^{(i)}_{t}=0\quad\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}a.s.
, so that the
i
th term disappears from (
B.93
) and (
B.94
) with probability
1
by the convention
0\log 0\coloneqq 0
.

Hence we have shown the convergence of:

\mathcal{C}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}-\mathrm{KL}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})),

point-wise in
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta
.

Next we show that
(\mathcal{C}_{t,n})_{n\geq 1}
are stochastically equicontinuous. Let
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
E\subset\Omega
such that
\mathbb{P}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}(E)=1
. Let
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\in\Theta
,
\omega\in E
, and
\varepsilon>0
. Firstly we will show the stochastic equicontinuity of
n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}
for any
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Let
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and write by the triangle inequality:

\displaystyle\left|n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}-n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|

(B.96)

\displaystyle+\left|n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|

(B.97)

\displaystyle+\left|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|.

(B.98)

There exists
N(\omega)<\infty
such that for
n>N(\omega)
the first two terms are bounded by
\varepsilon_{0}/3
by proposition
3
. Furthermore, since
\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
is continuous by lemma
16
there exists a
\delta_{0}>0
such that:

\left\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\right\|_{\infty}<\delta_{0}\implies\left|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f}\right|<\varepsilon_{0}/3.

Hence we have shown stochastic equicontinuity of
(n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{f})_{n\geq 1}
. Now, consider
\mathcal{C}_{t,n}
:

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{C}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})-\mathcal{C}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{i=1}^{m}n^{-1}y_{t}^{(i)}\log\frac{\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})}{\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})}\right|

(B.99)

\displaystyle+\left|n^{-1}\left[\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})^{\top}\right]\mathbf{1}_{m}\right|.

(B.100)

By what has been proven already we can choose
\delta_{1}
and
N_{1}(\omega)
to bound (
B.100
) by
\varepsilon/2
. Let
\varepsilon_{2}>0
, by proposition
1
there exists
N_{2}(\omega)
such that for
n>N_{2}(\omega)
:

\left|\sum_{i=1}^{m}n^{-1}y_{t}^{(i)}\log\frac{\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})}{\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})}\right|<\sum_{i=1}^{m}|\mu_{t,\infty}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})+\varepsilon_{2}|\left|\log\frac{n^{-1}\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})}{n^{-1}\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})}\right|

(B.101)

Furthermore, for each
i\in[m]
either:

•

there is positive probability that either
\mu^{(i)}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})=0
or
{\mu^{(i)}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})=0}
, then the
i
th term of the sum on the l.h.s. of (
B.101
) disappears since
y_{t}^{(i)}=0
with probability
1
by lemma
13
, and we invoke the convention
0\log 0\coloneqq 0
; or

•

{\mu^{(i)}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})>0}
and
\mu^{(i)}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})>0
almost surely. Then by continuity of
\log
on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
there exists a
\delta_{3}^{(i)}>0
such that for
|n^{-1}\mu^{(i)}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})-n^{-1}\mu^{(i)}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})|<\delta_{3}^{(i)}
:

\displaystyle\left|\log\frac{n^{-1}\mu^{(i)}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})}{n^{-1}\mu^{(i)}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\log n^{-1}\mu^{(i)}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})-\log{n^{-1}\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})}\right|

\displaystyle<\frac{\varepsilon}{2m|\mu_{t,\infty}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})+\varepsilon_{2}|}.

By stochastic equicontinuity of
(n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{f})_{n\geq 1}
there exists
N_{3}(\omega)
and
\delta_{2}
such that for

n>N_{3}(\omega)
and
\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|_{\infty}<\delta_{2}
we have that
\|n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})-n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\|_{\infty}<\min_{i}\delta_{3}^{(i)}
so that:

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i=1}^{m}n^{-1}y_{t}^{(i)}\log\frac{\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})}{\mu_{t,n}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})}\right|

\displaystyle<\sum_{i=1}^{m}|\mu_{t,\infty}^{*(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})+\varepsilon_{2}|\frac{\varepsilon}{2m|\mu_{t,\infty}^{*(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})+\varepsilon_{2}|}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon/2.

Hence choosing
\delta=\min(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
and
N(\omega)=\max(N_{1}(\omega),N_{2}(\omega),N_{3}(\omega))
we have that for

{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|_{\infty}<\delta}
and
n>N(\omega)
:

\left|\mathcal{C}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})-\mathcal{C}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\right|<\varepsilon/2+\varepsilon/2=\varepsilon.

Hence we have established the stochastic equicontinuity of
\mathcal{C}_{t,n}
. This along with the already proven pointwise convergence establishes uniform almost sure convergence by lemma
21
and completes the proof.
∎

B.3.2
Case (II)

We have:

\displaystyle n^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-n^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\Big\{

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\left[n^{-1}\bar{\mathbf{Y}}_{r}\odot\log\left({\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}\oslash{\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})}\right)\right]\mathbf{1}_{m}

\displaystyle+n^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\left[\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right]\mathbf{1}_{m}\Big\}.

Proposition 6
.

Let assumptions
1
-
4
hold. Then

n^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-n^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}-\sum_{r=1}^{R}\mathrm{KL}\left(\mathrm{Pois}\left[\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right]\|\mathrm{Pois}\left[\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]\right),

(B.102)

uniformly in
\boldsymbol{\theta}
.

Proof.

The details are similar to lemma
5
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
define the sequence of random functions
(\mathcal{D}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))_{n\geq 1}
,
\mathcal{D}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\coloneqq\sum_{r=1}^{R}\mathcal{D}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, where:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\left[n^{-1}\bar{\mathbf{Y}}_{r}\odot\log\left({\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}\oslash{\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})}\right)\right]\mathbf{1}_{m}

\displaystyle+n^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\left[\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right]\mathbf{1}_{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\bar{Y}_{r}^{(i,j)}\log\frac{M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}{M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})}+\left[M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right]

With the convention
0\log 0\coloneqq 0
.
To see that this mapping is almost surely well defined, consider the following cases for each
(i,j)\in[m]^{2}
. If both
M_{r,n}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})>0
, or
M_{r,n}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})>0

\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}\text{-}a.s.
, then the
\log
of each of these terms is almost surely well defined. If either
M_{r,n}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
, or
M_{r,n}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=0
with positive probability, then
\bar{Y}^{(i,j)}_{r}=0

\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}\text{-}a.s.
by lemma
17
and we invoke the convention
0\log 0\coloneqq 0
.

It is enough to show that for each
r\in\{1,\dots,R\}

\mathcal{D}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}-\mathrm{KL}\left(\text{Pois}\left[\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right]\|\text{Pois}\left[\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]\right),\text{ uniformly in }\boldsymbol{\theta}.

We show pointwise almost sure convergence and then stochastic equicontinuity. Fix
r\in\{1,\dots,R\}
and note that by proposition
4

n^{-1}\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta
and
r=1,\dots,R
. Furthermore by proposition
2

n^{-1}\bar{\mathbf{Y}}_{r}\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})
for all
r=1,\dots,R
. We claim that by the CMT:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\left[n^{-1}\bar{\mathbf{Y}}_{r}\odot\log\left({\mathbf{M}_{n,r}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}\oslash{\mathbf{M}_{n,r}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})}\right)\right]\mathbf{1}_{m}

(B.103)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle n^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\left[\mathbf{M}_{n,r}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mathbf{M}_{n,r}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right]\mathbf{1}_{m}

(B.104)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}n^{-1}\bar{Y}_{r}^{(i,j)}\log\frac{M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}{M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})}+n^{-1}\left[M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right]

(B.105)

\displaystyle\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}M^{(i,j)}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\log\frac{M^{(i,j)}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})}{M^{(i,j)}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})}+\left[M^{(i,j)}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})-M^{(i,j)}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right]

(B.106)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathrm{KL}(\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\|\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})).

(B.107)

To see that this limit is indeed almost surely well defined consider the cases for each
i=1,\dots,m
and

j\in[m]
, either:

•

M_{r,\infty}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})>0
and
M_{r,\infty}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})>0.
In this case all functions in the sequence and its limit are well defined. Or

•

M_{r,\infty}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})>0
and
M_{r,\infty}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=0
, or
M_{r,\infty}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})=0
and
M_{r,\infty}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})>0
. This case is prohibited by lemma
14
. Or

•

M_{r,\infty}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=M_{r,\infty}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=0
. In this case, by lemmas
13
and
15
, we have

M_{r,n}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=0
and
\bar{Y}^{(i,j)}_{r}=0\quad\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}a.s.
, so that the
(i,j)
th term disappears from the sums in (
B.105
) and (
B.106
) by the convention
0\log 0\coloneqq 0
.

Hence we have shown:

\mathcal{D}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}-\mathrm{KL}\left(\text{Pois}\left(\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\right)\|\text{Pois}\left(\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)\right),\text{ pointwise in }\boldsymbol{\theta}.

We now prove stochastic equicontinuity of
(\mathcal{D}_{r,n})_{n\geq 1}
. Firstly we will show the stochastic equicontinuity of
(n^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\boldsymbol{f}_{2})_{n\geq 1}
for any vectors
\boldsymbol{f}_{1},\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Let
\boldsymbol{f}_{1},\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
E\subseteq\Omega
such that
\mathbb{P}^{\theta^{*}}(E)=1
. Let
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\in\Theta
,
\omega\in E
, and
\varepsilon>0
. Let
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and write by the triangle inequality:

\displaystyle\left|n^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})\boldsymbol{f}_{2}-n^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|n^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})\boldsymbol{f}_{2}-\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\right|

\displaystyle+\left|n^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\boldsymbol{f}_{2}-\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\right|

\displaystyle+\left|\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})\boldsymbol{f}_{2}-\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\right|.

There exists
N(\omega)
such that for
n>N(\omega)
the first two terms are bounded by
\varepsilon_{0}/3
by proposition
3
. Furthermore, since
\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
is continuous by lemma
20
there exists a
\delta_{0}
such that:

\left\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\right\|_{\infty}<\delta_{0}\implies\left|\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})\boldsymbol{f}_{2}-\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\right|<\varepsilon_{0}/3.

Hence we have shown stochastic equicontinuity of
(n^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\boldsymbol{f}_{2})_{n\geq 1}
. Now, consider
\mathcal{D}_{r,n}
:

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{D}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})-\mathcal{D}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|n^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\left[\bar{\mathbf{Y}}_{r}\odot\log\left({\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})}\oslash{\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})}\right)\right]\mathbf{1}_{m}\right|

(B.108)

\displaystyle+\left|n^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{m}^{\top}\left[\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})-\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\right]\mathbf{1}_{m}\right|

(B.109)

By what has already been proven, for any
\varepsilon>0
we can choose
\delta_{1}
and
N_{1}(\omega)
to bound (
B.109
) by
\varepsilon/2
. Let
\varepsilon_{1}>0
, by proposition
2
there exists
N_{2}(\omega)
such that for
n>N_{2}(\omega)
,

\left|\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}n^{-1}\bar{Y}_{r}^{(i,j)}\log\frac{M_{r,n}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})}{M_{r,n}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})}\right|<\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}|M_{r,\infty}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})+\varepsilon_{1}|\left|\log\frac{n^{-1}M_{r,n}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})}{n^{-1}M_{r,n}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})}\right|.

Furthermore, for each
(i,j)\in[m]^{2}
either:

•

M_{r,n}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})=0
or
M_{r,n}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})=0
with positive probability. In this case the
(i,j)
th terms disappear from the sum on the left hand side since
\bar{Y}_{r}^{(i,j)}=0
with probability
1
by lemma
17
; or

•

M_{r,n}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})>0
and
M_{r,n}^{(i,j)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})>0
almost surely, by continuity of
\log
on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
there exists a
\delta_{3}^{(i,j)}>0
such that if
|M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})-M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})|<\delta^{(i,j)}_{3}
then:

\displaystyle\left|\log\frac{n^{-1}M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})}{n^{-1}M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\log{n^{-1}M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})}-\log{n^{-1}M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2m^{2}|M^{(i,j)}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})+\varepsilon_{1}|}.

Then by stochastic equicontinuity of
(n^{-1}\mathbf{M}_{r,n})_{n\geq 1}
there exists
N_{3}(\omega)
and
\delta_{2}
such that for
n>\max(N_{2}(\omega),N_{3}(\omega))
and
\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|_{\infty}<\delta_{2}
we have that

\|\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})-\mathbf{M}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\|_{\infty}<\min_{(i,j)}\delta^{(i,j)}_{3}
so that:

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}n^{-1}\bar{Y}^{(i,j)}_{r}\left|\log\frac{n^{-1}M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})}{n^{-1}M^{(i,j)}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})}\right|

\displaystyle<\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}|M^{(i,j)}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})+\varepsilon_{1}|\frac{\varepsilon}{2m^{2}|M^{(i,j)}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})+\varepsilon_{1}|}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon/2.

Choosing
\delta=\min(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
and
N(\omega)=\max(N_{1}(\omega),N_{2}(\omega),N_{3}(\omega))
we have that for
\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|_{\infty}<\delta
and
n>N(\omega)
:

\left|\mathcal{D}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})-\mathcal{D}_{r,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\right|<\varepsilon/2+\varepsilon/2=\varepsilon.

Hence we have established the stochastic equicontinuity of
(\mathcal{D}_{r,n})_{n\geq 1}
. This along with the already proven pointwise convergence establishes uniform almost sure convergence by lemma
21
.

∎

B.4
Convergence of Maximum PAL estimators

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
be defined to be the r.h.s. of (
B.91
) and let
\mathcal{C}_{n}
be as in the proof of proposition
5
. We have that
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n})\geq\mathcal{C}_{n}({\boldsymbol{\theta}})
for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta_{(I)}^{*}
. Furthermore
{\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})-\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})>0}
for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta
. We can combine these inequalities to obtain:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})-\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n})

(B.110)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})-\mathcal{C}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})+\mathcal{C}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})-\mathcal{C}_{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n})+\mathcal{C}_{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n})-\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n})

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta}\left|\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mathcal{C}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right|\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}0.

Hence
\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n})\underset{a.s.}{\overset{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}})
.

Now assume for purposes of contradiction that there is some positive probability that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}
does not converge to the set
\Theta^{*}_{(I)}
, i.e. assume that there is an event
E\subset\Omega
with
\mathbb{P}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}(E)>0
such that for all
\omega\in E
there exists a
\delta>0
such that for infinitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}(\omega)}
is not in the open neighbourhood
B_{\delta}(\Theta^{*})=\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta:\exists\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\in\Theta^{*}:\|\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\|<\delta\}
.
Since
\Theta
is compact, the set
B_{\delta}(\Theta_{(I)}^{*})^{c}=\Theta\setminus B_{\delta}(\Theta_{(I)}^{*})
is closed, bounded, and therefore compact. Furthermore,
\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
is continuous in
\boldsymbol{\theta}
. By the extreme value theorem this means that there exists a
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\in B_{\delta}(\Theta_{(I)}^{*})^{c}
such that for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in B_{\delta}(\Theta_{(I)}^{*})^{c}
:

\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\leq\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})

Furthermore, since
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\notin\Theta_{(I)}^{*}
there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that:

\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})<\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})-\varepsilon.

By our assumption we have for each
\omega\in E
there are infinitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}(\omega)\in B_{\delta}(\Theta_{(I)}^{*})^{c}
. But this implies that for each
\omega\in E
there are infinitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that:

\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}(\omega))\leq\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})<\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})-\varepsilon,

\implies|\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})-\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}(\omega))|>\varepsilon,

which contradicts (
B.110
). Hence we must have that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}
converges to the set
\Theta^{*}_{(I)}

\mathbb{P}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}
-a.s. The proof for case (II) follows the same arguments but with
\mathcal{C}_{n}
and
\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
replaced by
\mathcal{D}_{n}
as in the proof of proposition
6
and
\mathcal{D}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
defined to be the r.h.s. of (
B.102
).
∎

B.5
Identifiability

Proposition 7
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta^{*}_{(I)}

\displaystyle\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}),\quad\forall t=1,\ldots,T

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta^{*}_{(II)}

\displaystyle\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}),\quad\forall r=1,\ldots,R.

Proof.

For the first equivalence in the statement, in order to prove the implication in the forward direction, assume that
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta^{*}_{(I)}
, i.e.,
\quad\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})
, for all
t=1,\ldots,T
. Recall from the definitions in (
B.73
)-(
B.74
) that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{0,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
does not depend on
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
, hence neither does
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
, and so:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})^{\top}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}},\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\mathbf{q}_{1}({\boldsymbol{\theta}}))^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+{\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\mathbf{q}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+{\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}.

Now, for
t>1
assume that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})=\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
. Then we have that:

\displaystyle\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{m}-\mathbf{q}_{t-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle+\bigg(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}\bigg\{[(\mathbf{1}_{m}\otimes\mathbf{q}_{t-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))\odot\mathbf{G_{t-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}^{\top}]

\displaystyle\oslash\left[\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}\right]\bigg\}\bigg)^{\top}\bigg]\odot\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\left[1_{m}-\mathbf{q}_{t-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\mathbf{q}_{t-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]\odot\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta}),

so that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))}+\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))}+\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top},

and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})^{\top}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}},\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\mathbf{q}_{t}({\boldsymbol{\theta}}))^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+{\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\mathbf{q}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+{\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}.

By induction we have thus shown that
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})
for all
t=1,\dots,T
and have completed the proof for the forward direction of the first implication in the statement.

For the backwards direction we need to show that
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{\theta})\implies\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})
, for all
t=1,\ldots,T
. Similarly as for the forwards direction:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\mathbf{q}_{1}({\boldsymbol{\theta}}))^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+{\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\mathbf{q}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+{\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})^{\top}.

Now, for
t>1
assume that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t-1,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{\theta})=\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t-1,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{\theta})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})
. Then we have that:

\displaystyle\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{m}-\mathbf{q}_{t-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle+\bigg(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}\bigg\{[(\mathbf{1}_{m}\otimes\mathbf{q}_{t-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))\odot\mathbf{G_{t-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}^{\top}]

\displaystyle\oslash\left[\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}\right]\bigg\}\bigg)^{\top}\bigg]\odot\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\left[1_{m}-\mathbf{q}_{t-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\mathbf{q}_{t-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]\odot\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta}),

so that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))}+\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t-1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))}+\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top},

and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\mathbf{q}_{t}({\boldsymbol{\theta}}))^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+{\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\mathbf{q}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+{\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{t,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\top}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})^{\top}

This completes the proof of the first implication in the statement of the proposition.

For the second implication, we will first show
\mathbf{M}_{r\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}},\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathbf{M}_{r\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\implies\mathbf{M}_{r\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=\mathbf{M}_{r\infty}({\boldsymbol{\theta}},\boldsymbol{\theta})
, for all
r=1,\ldots,R
. Recalling the definitions in (
B.86
)-(
B.87
), we have that for all
s\in\{1,\dots,\tau_{1}\}
,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{s,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{s,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})
and hence:

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=\mathbf{M}_{1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{\tau_{1}}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{s,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\mathbf{Q}_{s}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

(B.111)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{\tau_{1}}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{s,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\mathbf{Q}_{s}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

(B.112)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{M}_{1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta}).

(B.113)

Now let
r\geq 1
and assume that, for all
s\in\{\tau_{r-1}+1,\dots,\tau_{r}\}
,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{s,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{s,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})
. Then:

\displaystyle\bar{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{\tau_{r},\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\left[\mathbf{1}_{m}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{m}-\mathbf{Q}_{\tau_{r}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]\odot{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{\tau_{r},\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})}{\mathbf{M}_{r,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})}\left[\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\tau_{r},\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\odot\mathbf{Q}_{\tau_{r}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\tau_{r},\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\tau_{r},\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta}).

This then implies that for all
s\in\{\tau_{r}+1,\dots,\tau_{r+1}\}
,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{s,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{s,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})
, which in turn implies, as in (
B.111
) that
\mathbf{M}_{r+1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})=\mathbf{M}_{r+1,\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta})
. The reverse direction follows by similar reasoning, as in mirroring the proof of the backwards direction of the first implication in the statement of the proposition, so the details are omitted.
∎

Appendix C
Supplementary material for section
7

C.1
Supplementary material for the pedagogical SEIR example

In this section we present the PALSMC algorithm used in the pedagogical SEIR example, given by algorithm
6
. For notation purposes, define
\mathbf{y}_{t}=[0\;0\;y_{t}\;0]
. The following section describes how one can make proposals informed by observations.

C.1.1
Deriving a proposal informed by observations

Let
f(\cdot|\mu_{q},\sigma^{2}_{q})
be the density associated with a
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{q},\sigma^{2}_{q})_{\geq 0,\leq 1}
random variable. We would like to make proposals informed by observations, to that end we seek a Laplace approximation to:

\hat{p}(q_{t}\mid y_{1:t},q_{1:t-1}):=\frac{\exp\ell(y_{t}\mid y_{1:t-1},q_{1:t})f(q_{t}|\mu_{q},\sigma^{2}_{q})}{\int\exp\ell(y_{t}\mid y_{1:t-1},q_{1:t})f(q_{t}|\mu_{q},\sigma^{2}_{q})dq_{t}}.

Suppressing dependence on the particle, let
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t}
be calculated as per line
4
of algorithm
6
. We have for some constant
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
:

\displaystyle\log\hat{p}(q_{t}|y_{t})

\displaystyle=\ell(y_{t}\mid y_{1:t-1},q_{1:t})+f(q_{t}|\mu_{q},\sigma^{2}_{q})+C_{1}

(C.1)

\displaystyle=y_{t}\log(q_{t})+y_{t}\log(\lambda^{(3)}_{t})-q_{t}-\log y_{t}!-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q_{t}-\mu_{q}}{\sigma_{q}}\right)^{2}+C_{2}

To get the mean of a Laplace approximation to (
C.1
) we must find it’s maximum w.r.t.
q_{t}
, hence:

\displaystyle\frac{d\log\hat{p}(q_{t}\mid y_{t})}{dq_{t}}

\displaystyle=\frac{y_{t}}{q_{t}}-\lambda^{(3)}_{t}-\frac{q_{t}-\mu_{q}}{\sigma_{q}^{2}}=0

(C.2)

\displaystyle\iff(q_{t})^{2}+(\lambda^{(3)}_{t}\sigma_{q}^{2}-\mu_{q})q_{t}-y_{t}\sigma_{q}^{2}=0

\displaystyle\implies q_{t}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mu_{q}-\lambda^{(3)}_{t}\sigma_{q}^{2}+\sqrt{(\lambda^{(3)}_{t}\sigma_{q}^{2}-\mu_{q})^{2}+4y_{t}\sigma_{q}^{2}}\right)=:\mu_{prop}

For the variance we find the second derivative and evaluate it at
\mu_{prop}
:

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\log\hat{p}(q_{t}\mid y_{t})}{d(q_{t})^{2}}=-\frac{y_{t}}{(q_{t})^{2}}-\frac{1}{\sigma_{q}^{2}}

(C.3)

\displaystyle\implies\sigma_{prop}^{2}=\left(\frac{y_{t}}{\mu_{prop}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{q}^{2}}\right)^{-1}.

To be congruent with the support of
q_{t}
we truncate the proposal to be
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{prop},\sigma_{prop}^{2})_{\geq 0,\leq 1}
, denote its density as
\pi(\cdot|\mu_{prop},\sigma_{prop}^{2})
.

Algorithm 6

PAL within SMC

1:

initialize:

\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{0,i}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{0}
for
i=1
to
n_{part}
.

2:

for

t\geq 1
:

3:

for

i=1,\dots,n_{part}
:

4:

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t}^{(i)}\leftarrow\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{t-1}^{(i)}\odot\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{K}_{t,\eta\left(\boldsymbol{\bar{\lambda}}_{t}^{(i)}\right)}+\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{t}

5:

q_{t}^{(i)}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\mu_{prop},\sigma_{prop}^{2}\right)_{\geq 0,\leq 1}
calculated as per
C.2
and
C.3
.

6:

\mathbf{q}_{t}^{(i)}\leftarrow[0\;0\;q_{t}^{(i)}\;0]^{\top}

7:

\log w_{t}^{(i)}\leftarrow\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}\log\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t}^{(i)}\odot\mathbf{q}_{t}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{q}_{t}^{(i)}-\log\mathbf{y}_{t}!+\log f(q_{t}^{(i)}|\mu_{q},\sigma_{q}^{2})-\log\pi(q_{t}^{(i)}|\mu_{prop},\sigma_{prop}^{2})

8:

\boldsymbol{\bar{\lambda}}_{t}^{(i)}\leftarrow\left(\mathbf{1}_{m}-\mathbf{q}_{t}^{(i)}\right)\odot\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t}^{(i)}+\mathbf{y}_{t}

9:

end for

10:

\ell(y_{t}\mid y_{1:t-1})\leftarrow\frac{1}{n_{part}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{part}}w_{t}^{(i)}

11:

\bar{w}_{t}^{(i)}\leftarrow w_{t}^{(i)}/\sum_{j=1}^{n_{part}}w_{t}^{(j)}

12:

resample

\left\{\boldsymbol{\bar{\lambda}}_{t}^{(i)},q_{t}^{(i)}\right\}_{i=1}^{n_{part}}
according to a systematic resampling scheme with weights
\left\{\bar{w}_{t}^{(i)}\right\}_{i=1}^{n_{part}}
.

13:

end for

C.1.2
Comparison to a standard sequential Monte Carlo approach

In this section we perform a routine comparison of PALSMC evaluations and ‘exact’ particle filter likelihood estimates on a small simulated model for which a standard SMC approach, that is integrating out both the
\mathbf{x}_{t}
and the
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}
processes, is still viable. The model we use is the same as in the pedagogical SEIR example, except we introduce over-dispersion into the infection rate, that is the number of new exposed at time
t
is distributed:

B_{t}\sim\mathrm{Bin}(S_{t},1-e^{-h\xi_{t}\beta\frac{I_{t}}{n_{t}}}),

(C.4)

where
\xi_{t}\sim\text{gamma}(\sigma_{\xi},\sigma_{\xi})
is mean
1
multiplicative noise. We simulated data using the same parameters as in the pedagogical SEIR example and
\sigma_{\xi}=1
. For the standard SMC approach we used joint proposals inspired by the PALSMC derivations.

We ran each of the filters with the data generating parameters as input with 1000, 5000, and 10000 particles. For each procedure and each particle size we ran generated 100 likelihood estimates and calculated the standard deviation. We found that, as expected, the variance shrinks for both procedures as the number of particles increases, and that PALSMC had a systematically lower variance with little bias.

C.2
A simulation example

Consider the following SEIR model with immigration and emigration:
\mathbb{P}_{0,n}=\mathrm{Mult}\left(n,\left[0.99\;\;0\;\;0.01\;\;0\right]^{\top}\right)
and for all
t
:
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{t,n}}=\left[\frac{4}{100}n\;\;\frac{4}{100}n\;\;\frac{4}{100}n\;\;\frac{4}{100}n\right]^{\top}
,
{\boldsymbol{\delta}_{t}}=\left[\frac{98}{100}\;\;\frac{98}{100}\;\;\frac{98}{100}\;\;\frac{98}{100}\right]^{\top}
,
{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{t,n}=\left[\frac{1}{100}n\;\;\frac{1}{100}n\;\;\frac{1}{100}n\;\;\frac{1}{100}n\right]^{\top}
,
{\mathbf{q}}_{t}=\left[0.1\;\;0.1\;\;0.3\;\;0.2\right]^{\top}

and

\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}e^{-\beta^{*}\eta^{(3)}}&1-e^{-\beta\eta^{(3)}}&0&0\\
0&e^{-\rho}&1-e^{-\rho}&0\\
0&0&e^{-\gamma}&1-e^{-\gamma}\\
0&0&0&1\end{array}\right],\quad{\mathbf{G}}_{t}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}0.95&0&0.05&0\\
0.3&0&0.7&0\\
0.15&0&0.85&0\\
0&0&0&1\\
\end{array}\right]

with DGP
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}=[\beta^{*}\;\rho^{*}\;\gamma^{*}]^{\top}=[0.5\;0.05\;0.1]^{\top}
.

This observation model can be interpreted as follows: with probability
q_{t}^{(i)}
each individual in compartment
i
is tested for disease. Allowing
q_{t}^{(i)}
to vary across
i
could model, for example, infective individuals being more likely to be tested. The above choice of
{\mathbf{G}}_{t}
allows for false-positives (first row) and false-negatives (third row), where those testing positive are considered infective, and those testing negative are considered susceptible. Of course, other choices are possible.

limits (bold line) for each population size (left to right),

. Middle two rows: filtering intensities associated with the

simulated data sets with

taken to be

. Bottom two rows: filtering with
\boldsymbol{\theta}
set erroneously
\beta=0.1
,

, and all other parameters set as for the middle two rows.

7
show
n^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{t}
and
n^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{t}
simulated
50
times from the model with population sizes
n\in\{100,1000,10000,100000\}
. Note that in the top row, the fact that trajectories for compartment
S
in
n^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{t}
are valued above
1
in places is explained in terms of immigration into the
S
compartment exceeding the combined effect of emigration from
S
and individuals transitioning from
S
to
E
. With
n=100
, the fact that some trajectories for the
S
compartment are roughly increasing over time corresponds to the lack of an outbreak; for other trajectories which rise and then fall, an outbreak does occur.

Due to the choices of
\mathbb{P}_{0,n}
,
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{t,n}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{t,n}
set out above, it is immediate that the vectors
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{0,\infty}
,
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{t,\infty}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}_{t,\infty}
appearing in assumptions
4
and
2
exist. The convergence of
n^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{t}
and
n^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{t}
as
n\to\infty
to deterministic limits as discussed in section
5.3
is evident in figure
7
.

The middle two rows of figure
7
show the behaviour of the scaled filtering intensities
n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
and
n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
obtained from algorithm
1
in the case of correctly specified parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
. It is evident that, as per the discussion of asymptotic filtering accuracy in section
5.3
, as
n\to\infty
these quantities converge to the same deterministic limits as do
n^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{t}
and
n^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{t}
, respectively. On the other hand, as illustrated in the bottom two rows of figure
7
, when the model is not correctly specified, then
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t,n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
converge to limits which are not equal to the limits of
n^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{t}
and
n^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{t}
.

. Vertical black dashed lines are the maximum PAL estimates for each surface, the vertical red line is the DGP. The two rows show the same 3-d plots from different viewing angles.

illustrates the behaviour of the scaled log-PAL

evaluated over a find grid of values fo

(all other parameters held constant). Each purple surface in each plot corresponds to a different data set simulated from the model, as in the second row of figure

. As
n
grows, figure

evidences convergence of the maximum PAL estimates to the true parameter value, as per theorem
1
.

C.3
Delayed Acceptance PMCMC for the boarding school influenza outbreak

This example illustrates the use of the PAL within delayed acceptance PMCMC, specifically the delayed acceptance Particle Marginal Metropolis Hastings (daPMMH) algorithm of
[
26
]
.

Data and model

The data set is the well-known boarding school influenza outbreak data, recorded at a British boarding school in 1978 and reported in the British Medical Journal
[
6
,
17
]
. The data are available in the R package “pomp”
[
39
]
. On day one there was one infection and over the course of the
14
day epidemic a total of
512
students reported symptoms from a population of
n=763
. The observations are prevalence data: daily counts of the total number of symptomatic individuals. We cast this an instance of case (I), using a simple SIR model, where the initial state of the population is fixed to
\left[763\;1\;0\right]^{\top}
and we define the matrix
\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}}
as follows:

\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}e^{-\beta\eta^{(2)}}&1-e^{-\beta\eta^{(2)}}&0\\
0&e^{-\gamma}&1-e^{-\gamma}\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right],

where
\beta
and
\gamma
are to be estimated. Observations
y_{t}
are modelled as binomially under-reported counts of infected individuals, that is, given
x_{t}^{(2)}
,
y_{t}\sim\text{Bin}(x_{t}^{(2)},q)
where
q\in[0,1]
is unknown and to be estimated. To connect with the notation of algorithm
1
we have
\mathbf{y}_{t}\equiv[0\;y_{t}\;0]^{\top}
and
\mathbf{q}_{t}\equiv[0\;q\;0]^{\top}
for
t\geq 1
.

Delayed Acceptance Particle Marginal Metropolis Hastings

In the standard PMMH algorithm
[
5
]
, one calculates a particle filter approximation to the likelihood for each proposed parameter value, which is typically a computationally intensive operation. The daPMMH algorithm introduces an additional ‘pre-screening’ acceptance step based on an approximate likelihood which is assumed to be cheap to evaluate. Only if the proposed parameter is accepted in this initial step is a particle filter approximation to the likelihood then evaluated; thus in performing this additional step, one seeks to avoid running a particle filter for proposals which are likely to be rejected. Details of the validity of the scheme, in the sense that it indeed targets the true posterior distribution over the parameters, can be found in
[
26
]
. Algorithm
7
in section
C.3.1
of the supplementary material illustrates how to use a PAL within a daPMMH.

We stress that, although for the SIR model the number of compartments is small (
m=3
), and for the data set in question the population size is fairly small (
n=763
), this actually presents a stern relative speed test for PALs versus particle filters: the particle filter element of the daPMMH and PMMH algorithms involves simulating from the latent compartmental model, and the overall cost of the particle filter, therefore, grows with both the number of compartments and the size of the population, as well as the number of particles. By contrast, evaluating the PAL involves no random number generation and has a cost independent of population size. Thus, if a relative speed gain using PALs can be demonstrated with a small population size and small number of compartments, it is reasonable to expect an even greater relative speed gain for models with larger numbers of compartments and larger populations.

Results

We compare the performance of three algorithms: PALMH: a Metropolis-within-Gibbs algorithm with the PAL substituted in place of the exact likelihood, i.e., targeting an approximation to the exact posterior distribution; PMMH: a standard Particle Marginal Metropolis-Hastings within Gibbs; daPMMH: a delayed acceptance Particle Marginal Metropolis-Hastings within Gibbs, in which we use the PAL for the delayed acceptance step. We apply these three methods to both a synthetic and a real dataset.
For all three algorithms we use Gaussian random walk proposals independently for each element of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
. The random walk variances are tuned to ensure acceptance rates between 20% and 40%. The PMMH and daPMMH algorithms were each run with
1000
particles. All experiments were run on a single core of a 1.90 GHz i7-8650U CPU.

The parameters of the model are collected in the vector
\boldsymbol{\theta}=[\beta\;\gamma\;q]^{\top}
. We consider a fairly vague prior
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})=p(\beta)p(\gamma)p(q)
, where
p(\beta)
and
p(\gamma)
are truncated Gaussian densities
\mathcal{N}(0,1)_{\geq 0}
and
p(q)
is a truncated Gaussian density
\mathcal{N}(0.5,0.5)_{\geq 0,\leq 1}
.

Simulated data.

We simulated an epidemic for 14 days with the parameter regime
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}=[\beta^{*}\;\gamma^{*}\;q^{*}]^{\top}=[2\;0.5\;0.8\;]^{\top}
. For each of the PALMH, PMMH, and daPMMH we ran a
5\times 10^{5}
length chain, discarded
10^{5}
for burn in and then thinned to a sample of
2.5\times 10^{5}
. Trace plots, autocorrelation plots, and posterior sample histograms for each scheme are presented in section
C.3.1
of the supplementary material, the rates of decay of the ACFs with respect to lag for the daPMMH and PMMH algorithms are similar, the rate of decay for the PALMH algorithm is faster. The Monte Carlo approximations of the posterior marginals are closely matched across the three algorithms, see table
5
for summary statistics, and are concentrated around the data generating parameters. A single evaluation of the PAL took a mean time of
9.4\times 10^{-6}
seconds, the particle filter approximation to the likelihood took a mean time of
4.5\times 10^{-3}
seconds, both algorithms were implemented with Rcpp.

Real data.

On the real data we ran the PALMH, PMMH, and daPMMH for
5\times 10^{5}
iterations each, with run times of 12.2 minutes, 4.5 hours, and 2.8 hours respectively, exhibiting the speed benefits of the PAL approach. Trace plots, autocorrelation plots, and approximate posterior sample histograms for each scheme are presented in section
C.3.1
, the rate of decay of the ACF with lag is similar for the daPMMH and PMMH algorithms, the rate of decay for the PALMH algorithm is faster. The daPMMH and PMMH algorithms yield very similar approximate posterior marginals as expected – see table
6
. The posterior marginals obtained from the PALMH scheme exhibit modes in different locations to those from PMMH/daPMMH, with the following epidemiological interpretation. The approximate posterior marginals obtained from PALMH correspond to a fast growing outbreak (large
\beta
), with individuals spending longer in the infected state (small
\gamma
) and a relatively lower reporting rate (relatively small
q
). By contrast, the PMMH/daPMMH marginals suggest a slower outbreak (smaller
\beta
) with less time spent in the infected compartment (larger
\gamma
), but with a higher case reporting rate (relatively high
q
).
Posterior predictive checks
[
24
]
show that, while having contrasting epidemiological interpretations (potentially due to model mis-specification), both PALMH and PMMH/daPMMH achieve good coverage of the data, see figure
9
. The mean trajectories from these posterior predictive distributions reflect the above interpretations of posterior marginals. The posterior predictive means and credible regions were calculated from
10000
samples from the posterior predictive distributions produced by the PALMH and daPMMH respectively; each sample from the posterior predictive distribution was generated by: sampling a parameter
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}
from the approximate posterior; then using
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}
to simulate an epidemic trajectory and data record from the model.

We performed inference on this data set using a Linear Noise Approximation to the likelihood as described in section 4 of
[
21
]
within a Metropolis Hastings scheme, the full results can be found in the supplementary material. We find that, whilst the LNA and the PAL perform similarly in terms of parameter inference, the Latent Compartmental Model from which the PAL is derived is more congruent with reality than the SDE model, since the latter allows non-integer and negative counts of individuals in compartments. Furthermore, a single evaluation of the PAL was approximately
\sim 100
times faster than a single evaluation of the LNA marginal likelihood for this dataset.

PALMH: prior sensitivity analysis

Section
C.3
explores Bayesian analysis on real data under vague priors, with results in table
6
. Whilst both the PALMH and PMMH schemes result in identical inferences on simulated data, there are discrepancies on the real-world boarding school data - which could be attributed to a misspecified model. The estimated parameters under the PALMH suggest an
R_{0}
of around
7.3
(the PMMH estimates suggest an
R_{0}
of around
4
) which is consistent with the entire population being infected at some point during the epidemic - one can question whether this is a realistic inference. Given the closed nature of this epidemic, along with the likelihood of close monitoring of the individuals in the system, one could afford to place stronger priors on
q
. In this section, we explore the inferences one can make using the PALMH scheme under more informative priors. We consider the following scenarios:

1.

\mathcal{N}(0.5,0.5)_{\geq 0,\leq 1}
- the vague prior used in the original analysis.

2.

\text{Beta}(9,1)
- an informative prior with mean
0.9
and variance
0.0082
and mode
1
.

3.

\text{Beta}(95,5)
- a strongly informative prior with mean
0.95
and variance
0.00047
and mode
1
.

4.

\delta_{0.9}
- an atomic prior on
0.9
(the mean inferred
q
under the PMMH analysis).

5.

\delta_{0.95}
- an atomic prior on
0.95
.

For each of these we ran a
5\times 10^{5}
length chain, discarded
10^{5}
for burn in and then thinned to a sample of
2.5\times 10^{5}
, we summarise our findings in table
7
. We find that as the prior belief in a high reporting rate is strengthened, the resulting estimated
R_{0}
lowers. If one places strong prior belief in a high reporting rate, see the
\text{Beta}(95,5)
and
\delta_{0.95}
columns, then the inferred
R_{0}
falls in line with our findings using the PMMH procedure.

posterior mean point estimates.

To investigate the disparity between inferences using the PALMH procedure vs the PMMH procedure when applied to real data, exhibited in table
6
, we repeated the analysis with a fixed
q=0.9
(equivalent to the
\delta_{0.9}
prior). The resulting posteriors for the PALMH and PMMH procedures still exhibited some differences, but were much more similar as a result of this stronger assumption:

•

The prosterior mean and 95% credible interval for
\beta
under the PMMH procedure was 2.14 (1.91,2.40), to be compared with 2.46( 2.08,2.92) for PALMH.

•

The prosterior mean and 95% credible interval for
\gamma
under the PMMH procedure was 0.58 (0.53,0.64), to be compared with 0.55( 0.48,0.63) for PALMH.

•

The posterior mean estimates for
R_{0}
under the PMMH and PALMH procedures were 4.52 and 3.70, respectively.

C.3.1
Algorithm details for section
C.3

The following algorithm describes how the PAL can be used within a delayed acceptance pmcmc scheme.

Algorithm 7

Delayed acceptance PMMH algorithm with PAL

1:

Initialize:

i=0
, set
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0}
arbitrarily.

2:

Run a particle filter to produce an approximation to
p(\mathbf{y}_{1:t}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0})
and denote this as
\hat{p}(\mathbf{y}_{1:t}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0})
.

3:

Run algorithm
1
to produce a PAL approximation to
p(\mathbf{y}_{1:t}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0})
and denote this as
\hat{p}_{a}(\mathbf{y}_{1:t}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0})
.

4:

for

i\geq 1:

5:

sample
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}\sim q(\cdot\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-1}).

6:

stage 1

•

Run algorithm
1
to produce a PAL approximation to
p(\mathbf{y}_{1:t}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*})
and denote this as
\hat{p}_{a}(\mathbf{y}_{1:t}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*})
.

•

With probability:

\alpha_{1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*})=\min\left\{1,\frac{\hat{p}_{a}(\mathbf{y}_{1:t}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*})p(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*})}{\hat{p}_{a}(\mathbf{y}_{1:t}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-1})p(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-1})}\frac{q(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-1}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*})}{q(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-1})}\right\},

run a particle filter to produce an approximation to
p(\mathbf{y}_{1:t}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*})
, denote this as
\hat{p}(\mathbf{y}_{1:t}\mid\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*})
and go to
Stage 2
. Otherwise, set
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-1}
, set
i=i+1
and return to 4.

7:

stage 2

With probability

\alpha_{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*})=\min\left\{1,\frac{\hat{p}(\mathbf{y}_{1:t}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*})p(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*})}{\hat{p}(\mathbf{y}_{1:t}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-1})p(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-1})}\frac{\hat{p}_{a}(\mathbf{y}_{1:t}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-1})p(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-1})}{\hat{p}_{a}(\mathbf{y}_{1:t}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*})p(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*})}\right\},

set
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}
, otherwise set
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-1}
. Set
i=i+1
and return to 4.

8:

end for

Time comparisons with the Linear Noise Approximation

For comparisons with the PAL we consider LNAMH: a Metropolis-within-Gibbs algorithm with a Linear noise approximation (LNA) to the likelihood of a stochastic differential equation model used in the accept/reject step, see
[
21
]
for details. We apply the LNAMH to the real dataset to compare and contrast to the PAL, for these comparisons we implement the PAL in base R, whereas the LNA computations use base R interfaced with fortran for cumbersome ODE solving calculations. The LNAMH implementation introduces an extra parameter in the variance of a Gaussian obsevation model, analogous to
V(\boldsymbol{\theta})
in section 4.2 of
[
21
]
, which we will denote as
v
; we consider a vague truncated Gaussian prior of
\mathcal{N}(400,300)_{\geq 0}
. We ran the chain for 100k iterations, discarded the first 20k and thinned to a sample of 25k to produce the posterior histograms.

The posterior predictive plot associated with the LNAMH sample, figure
10
, demonstrates good coverage of the data, yet they help illustrate some of its shortfalls in comparison to the PAL approach: the Gaussian nature of the ingredients of the LNA permits non-integer and even allows negative valued observations, which is clearly not parsimonious with reality; further, modelling with a constant in time observation variance leads to underconfidence in the start and end of the data record. In order to circumvent these issues within the LNA framework, one would have to turn to sophisticaed and expensive methods; alternatively, one could avoid each of them for free through the use of PALs.

Figure 10:
Posterior predictive distribution for LNAMH sample. To produce this plot we sampled a parameter from the approximate posterior and simulated from the SDE model 1000 times.

Figure
11
reports the mean time ratio between a single evaluation of the LNA likelihood and a single evaluation of the PAL for varying ODE solver intermediate time step choices for the LNA and analogous choice of
h
for the PAL. The order of magnitude of the speed gains is around 100 for the PAL, demonstrating the significant speed benefits given by the simplicity of computations needed to compute the PAL in comparison to cumbersome ODE solution calculations. Experiments were performed on an Intel(R) Core(TM) i7-7700HQ CPU @ 2.80GHz processor.

Figure 11:
Time comparisons for the LNA. The ratio of one evaluation of the LNA liklihood to one evaluation of the PAL for varying ODE solver intermediate time steps, with the comparative PAL evaluation run with
h=1/\penalty\text{number of timesteps}
. Percentiles are based on 1000 runs.

\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}=(\beta^{*},\gamma^{*},q^{*})=(2,0.5,0.8)
. The plots display the first
10^{5}
iterations after the burn in period.

.

C.4
Supplementary material for section
7.1

To write the age structured model of section
7.1
as an instance of the Latent Compartmental Model we take
m=16
and identify vectors
\mathbf{x}_{k,t}:=\left[S_{k,t}\;E_{k,t}\;I_{k,t}\;R_{k,t}\right]^{\top}
,
\mathbf{x}_{t}:=\left[\mathbf{x}_{1,t}^{\top}\;\dots\;\mathbf{x}_{4,t}^{\top}\right]^{\top}
and matrices:

\mathbf{Z}_{k,t}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}S_{k,t}-B_{k,t}&B_{k,t}&0&0\\
0&E_{k,t}-C_{k,t}&C_{k,t}&0\\
0&0&I_{k,t}-D_{k,t}&D_{k,t}\\
0&0&0&R_{k,t}\end{array}\right],\;\mathbf{Z}_{t}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mathbf{Z}_{1,t}&&\dots&0\\
&\mathbf{Z}_{2,t,}&&\vdots\\
\vdots&&\ddots&\\
0&\dots&&\mathbf{Z}_{4,t}\end{array}\right].

\mathbf{K}_{k,t,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{x}_{t})}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}e^{-h\bar{\beta}_{k,t}}&1-e^{-h\bar{\beta}_{k,t}}&0&0\\
0&e^{-h\rho}&1-e^{-h\rho}&0\\
0&0&e^{-h\gamma}&1-e^{-h\gamma}\\
0&0&0&1\end{array}\right],\;\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mathbf{K}_{1,t,\boldsymbol{\eta}}&&\dots&0\\
&\mathbf{K}_{2,t,\boldsymbol{\eta}}&&\vdots\\
\vdots&&\ddots&\\
0&\dots&&\mathbf{K}_{4,t,\boldsymbol{\eta}}\end{array}\right],

where the
\bar{\beta}_{k,t}
are the elements of the vector on the l.h.s. of (
7.1
). Due to the block-diagonal structure of the matrix
\mathbf{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\eta}}
for this example, algorithm
3
can be simplified to avoid performing various multiplications by zero. The resulting procedure is algorithm
8
.
